Gll5O FIL McDOUGALL's Pategt SELF-RAISING FLOUR The Examiner, Saturday, September 22, 1906 THE FIRE OPAL. ie,

f all Nations.

The Government had cleared the Cabinet of company directors and that was something to be thankful for. No man who was affected with that" guinea-pigism " which was so prevalent in the late Government, served under Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman. A scheme of rewards for dock-yard men had been inaugurated. The men were to be encOnraged to make suggestions and would receive bonuses according to results. Several alterations of importance to post office workers had also been made, the learners being placed in a much better position. Mr. Howes observed that the labour difficulty in this country could be solved by total abstinence and co-operation. He would not give power to Govern- ments—he would give power to the people in the locali- ties. It was a glorious thing to find John Burns in his present position. However some extreme men might think proper to denounce him, it was much better to see a labourer placed in an official position than to have such positions monopolised by the aristocrats. There was no chance of labour rising to the position it ought to hold when it" slanged" its own comrades when they were placed in high offices. With regard to the army, Mr. Howes contended that viewing the position of the various nations, there never was a time in the history of our own country whenthere was a grander opportunity for the reduction of military armaments. He deprecated I the alarm which some people were stirring up as to I German intentions, and said the present was not the time to make blood-thirsty speeches,but to talk of peace and reconciliation. It was, he considered, a step forward thattaccording to Mr. Haldane's speech, the Governm ant had decided that the business side of the army's affairs should be in the hands of civilians. That was no doubt the outcome of the South African scandals and the great waste of money there. An Axperiment by the Government which was to be commended was the allot- ment of small holdings on Crown lands in Cambridge- shire. In conclusion Hr. Howes urged his hearers to work in order that they might retain the seat which they won so splendidly. They did not want Warrington to lapse back into Toryism, for a great centre of industry like this ought to know that its whole future was bound up with progress. Probably they would soon be faced with the acute question of the right and inclination of the House of Lords to obstruct legisla- tion. The increased activity which the House of Lords was manifesting, necessitated increased watchfulness on the part of the democracy. If the Lords insisted on wrecking progressive measures there would arise a great constitutional question which could , be solved, although it would need determination, patience and enterprise to do so. The Liberal party would not shirk the question, if challenged to the issue. The Education Bill probably did not satisfy all of them. The Education Minister had made concessions to certain portions of the community for the sake of peace which those people did not seem to be over-thankful for. But a Bill which established the right of public control and took away all unfair impositions upon teachers must be supported. (Applause). When the other great Bills came on they must also be supported, not because they were perfect but because they contained some perfect parts which would improve the condition cf the people. (Applause.) On the motion of Mr. S. WRIGHT seconded by Mr. J. EL WELL a vote of thanks was accorded to the speakers.

No. I. The English Girl.

n moving a similar compliment to the chairman, Mr. W. FURNESS said he fait certain that the Progressive Labour League would be one of the greatest factors in the development of the social and political life of the town. Mr. F. ATIMNSON seconded and the vote was heartily passed. The Chairman's reply concluded the meeting.

The Menaces of Modern Existence.—According to Sir MADGE. James Crichtcn-Browne, all those ardent motorists who do not come to a violent death, will end their days in a lunatic asylum. That is what the speed mania must inevitably lead to, but in these days when we have OUT health so much on our nerves, and we hear so continu- ally about the menaces of modern existence we are apt to regard these warnines vary much like the cry of " wolf, wolf," in the 'fable. No doubt Sir James Crichton-Browne had much to say that was useful in his address to the Sanitary Engineers' Conference,but whilst special dangers menace the motorist, Dr. Hyslop attached scarcely less importance to the injury to health inflicted by noise from which W'3 all alike must suffer. Carlyle and Herbert Spencer used to stuff their ears with cotton wool, and John Leech was killed, it is said, by barrel- organs, but according to the doctors brain unrest is ' much more prevalent in these days than at any former time. The roar and noise of city life contributes to it, whilst if we move into the country there are the cock- crowings, the dogs barking and the " greetings " of the cows to disturb our rest at night. Altogether there seems little hope for us, and no doubt an eminent doctor was right when he said that much of what we call the progress of civilisation, is simply the progress of insanity. The Duke of Rutland and Belvoir Cas.—Belvoir Castle, which it is reported the Duke of Rutland intends shutting up in consequenee of heavy suocession duties, is the ancestral home of the Manners, and has been uninterruptedly occupied by the family since it was rebuilt in 1563. There are something like -70,000 acres belonging to the estate, which twenty years ago brought in nearly £lOO,OOO a year. Probably this has been much diminished, or else it would tile difficult to understand the Duke's necessity for shutting up Belvoir which is one of the most famous centres in the hunting counties. It was there that the present Duke and Duchess—then Lord and Lady Granby—spent a good deal of their time in their early married days, and their four children, three daughters and an only son, are equally familiar with the Castle and its surroundings. Fashiens in Jewellery.—Fringes and tassels of pearls or diamonds are the latest notions among jewellers, but these ideas will be quickly annexed by dealers and workers in artificial gems, and therefore the mode is not likely to remain long in favour. At the moment pearls and diamonds are preferred, rubies and emeralds come next, then amethysts. Sapphires have, for the time being disappeared, and opals are much less in evidence than they were a few years ago. The long chains continue to be worn, and these are usually built of platinum, set with precious stones and small plaques of enamel. Fur Stoles.—ln the matter ef stoles there is little difference between the present models and those of a year ago. They are a trifle under and longer, and alas, heads as well as tails are used to ornament them. There is something very barbaric about the beads, with their inevitable rows of tiny white teeth and beady eyes, and the really nice woman will refuse to allow them to find a place upon any of her fur wraps. All fur stoles fall straight in front, it being no longer deemed correct to. throw one end over the shoulder.

-- The vogue for Velvet. During the corning winter velvet promises to be the leading fabric, both for indoor toilettes, and outdoor costumes,—Apart from •" moleskin" which is enjoying so great a vogue in Paris :just now dark hues will take the place of white cream :and pale colours, and red of a shade best described as pomegranate, seems likely to stand high in favour. A RAILWAY WORKER'S TRIALS. Suffered from Piles for Seventeen

Years: Cured by Bile Beans.

Amid the din and bustle of worshop life many a man toils in pain from week-end to week-end, and many pathetic stories are told of quiet devotion to duty in face of bodily torture. Just such a case is that of Mr. Walter Weaver, an employ 4 in the L. & N.-W. Railway Company's works at Crewe. When a local pressman visited Mr. Weaver recently at 5, llungerford Avenue, Crewe, he elicited the following story of his long suffering and his cure by Bile Beans : " For the past 17 years," said Mr. Weaver, "1 have been a victim to bleeding piles. The origin of my suffering was an unusually severe cold, which laid me up for several weeks. I suffered the most excruciating pain, especially during the last few years. Often I was unable to work, and have had to obtain sick leave for ten days at a time. At times I was neither able to sit down, lie in bed, or to walk about without enduring almost unbearable agony. I was medically attended,but found no lasting relief until I tried Bile Beans. After taking the Beans consistently for a short time, I felt considerable relief. Bile Beans at length completely rid meof the terrible piles, and all the chdflug,irritating pains were banished. I should think no man can have suffered more from piles than I have done and I am convinced that if other sufferers will try Bile Beans they will find them as beneficial as I did." Mr. Weaver's cure is a remarkable one, yet is typical of many such performed by Bile Beans. No matter of how long standing the trouble may be, there is always hope if Bile Beans are taken. Piles are undoubtedly caused by digestive troubles and constipation, and it is obviously absurd to expect local applications or removal by operation to effect a permanent cure. Bile Beans are a rational remedy for piles, for they restore Natures own perfect processes of digestion, assimilation,and secretion, thus remo*ing the congestion of the hcemorrhoidal veins, ,which is the direct cause of piles. Sufferers from piles should promptly seek the only rational remedy and take Bile Beans, the medicine which proved so efficacious in Mr. Weaver's seemingly hopeless case. REVISION COURT.

Mr. Clement Elphinitone Lloyd, barrister-at-law, attended in the large room at Grappenhall, on Monday morning, for thil purpose of revising the lists of voters for theGrappendall pollinf, district, which includes Latchford Without, Grappeghall, and Warrington (that part within the geographical county of Chester). Mr. Joseph smith, of Knutsford, represented the Liberals, and Mr. G. Duckworth and Mr. J. Dean, also of Knutsford, the Conservatives. There were also present Mr. S. J. Woodcock (assistant overseer for Latchford Without), and Mr. Lawrence (representing the assistant overseer for Warrington). There were five ownership claims, two lodger claims, two occupiers' claims, and one parochial claim. There was no objec- tion. APPLETON AND STOCKTON HEATH. Mr. Lloyd attended at the Hall,Cafe onMonday after- noon for the purpose of revising the voters' lists for Appleton polling district (Appleton-with-Hull and Stretton), and the Stockton Heath polling district (Stockton Heath, Walton Inferior and Walton Superior). The Conservatives were represented by Messrs. Duck- worth and J. Dean, and Mr. H. J. Westbrook, solicitor, and the Liberals by Mr. Smith. The assistant overseers present were :—ME. E. Bradshaw (Stockton Heath), Mr. Ellison (Stratton), Mr. Moulsdale (Walton Superior), and Mr. iVhitfield (Walton Inferior).—Nothing of public interest transpired. The Revising Barrister congratu- lated Mr. Whitfield on the condition of his lists, remarking that there was not a single objection.

DARESBURY AND WHITLEY. OVERSEER OBJECTED TO. Mr. Lloyd held a court on Tuesday at the Daresbnry Sessions Room for the revision of the voters' lists for the polling districts of Diresbury (Acton Grange, Daresuury, Hatton, Kekewich, Moore, Newton-by- Daresbury, Preston-on-the-Hill), and Whitley (Antrobus Seven Oaks, Whitley Inferior and Whitley Superior). Messrs. Duckworth And Dean again repres,ented the Conservatives, and Mr. Smith the Liberals. The assistant overseers present were Messrs. Lawton (Acton Grange), Hewitt (Hatton). Booth (Daresbnry), De Prez (Moore), Weir (Kekewick), Wilkinson (Seven Oaks and Whitley), Coppack (Antro bus), E. Clare (Preston Brook), and Wright (Newton). Mr. Smith objected to the vote of Mr. Fryer, who was an overseer for Kekewick.

It was pointed out that the notices of objection stated to have been sent to Mr. Fryer as the person objected to, and to him as the overseer, were not produced. Mr. Smith : But, sir, you have held that when the assistant overseer has neglected— The Revising Barrister : This is not neglect, He has not received _the notice. _

Mr. Smith : I say I sent one to Mr: Fryer as the person objected to, and one to him also as overseer. Mr. Duckworth : Then produce the duplicate of that you sent to the overseer. Mr. Smith: This man is the overseer, and he received the objection. The Barrister: We don't know that ; you have not proved it. After further remarks, The Barrister said it was scarcely probable than an overseer would petition against himself. The objection was not held good. At the conclusion of the Court the Revilng Barrister was thanked for his services on the m,o .ion of Mi. Smith, seconded by Mr. Duckworth.

RAISTNG MONEY ON THE "ZORPSp."

William Johnson, a fireman, of 11, York-street, waa found guilty of a somewhat cold-blooded sort of fraud at St. Helens on Tuesday. Johnson is a widower, and has one child, Eliza, aged eleven years. Being on the spree, Johnson went on Monday to the undertaking establishment of Mr. F. Roden,Haydock-street, and said that his child Eliza, aged twelve, was lying dead at 10, York-street. He gave Mrs. Roden the order for the funeral, and said he had £8 club money to draw on the following day. 'On the strength of this Johnson borrowed a shilling. Mrs. Roden at once sent the undertaken to 10, York-street, who somewhat upset the residents by stating that he had come to "measure the corpse." Johnson in the meantime had spent the shilling, and four hours after his first visit he went to Mrs. Roden to borrow another 2s. She told him that her man had been, but there was no corpse,and Johnson was given in charge of Police-constable Herton.—John- son said he was in the horrors of drink, or he would not have done it. He would repay the shilling. Fined 215., or twenty-one days.

PROGRESSIVE LABOUR LEAGUE.

POLITICAL SITUATION REVIEWED.

Speeches by Mr. F. Neilson and Mr. J. Howes.

The Progressive Labour League inaugurated its winter session on Monday evening with a public meet- ing in the Brick-street Mission. There was a large attendance, presided over by Mr. Thomas Barnes, and the principal speakers were Mr. Fred Neilson, late Liberal candidate for the Newport Division, and Mr. Joseph Howes. Amongst others on the platform were :—Messrs. W. Furness, S. Wright, J. Elwell, F. Atkinson, and J. Morton (secretary of the League). .. The CHAIRICkN emphasised the value of propaganda work, and pointed out that since their great defeat at the General Election theConservativeparty had formed a Propaganda Club ill Manchester, with Mr. Chamberlain as president. It behoved Progressives to be on the alert and continue the educational work in the constitu- encies, and it was indeed a pleasing sign that working- men were beginning to take such a great interest in the conduct of the affairs of the nation. (Applause). Mr. FRED NEILSON,CChO had a very cordial reception, said that when he last spoke to them in Warrington, it was at a very remarkable meeting held in the Parr Hall when his good friend Mr. Leonard Outhwaite explained the matter of Chinese labour.At that time,he supposed, perhaps the most sanguine of their townsmen would not have predicted a 1500 majority for Mr. Crosfleld. They had, he dare say, had many congratulations showered upon them for that very notable triumph and he might be rather late in the day in offering hie. The victory must be attributed to the wonderful work of propaganda and organization that had been done in the constituency for several years before. Mr. Howes was second to none in England as an organizer and the members of that League must have worked loyally and faithfully with him to the close of the poll. He (Mr. Neilson) thought they were going to do wonderful things in Birmingham. They did not, as far as the poll was concerned, but if his henrers scrutinised the figures they would find tbat there were 4,000 abstentions—a very curious thing for Birmingham. Let 1 the Liberals lay the flattering unction to their souls that I perhaps some of the work they did in Birmingham ' remained in the minds of those absentees and that they could not therefore vote for MaChamberlain and Protec- tion, but perhaps there had not bean sufficient propa- ganda work and organization to get them to vote for Mr. Onthwaite and Free Trade. Mr. Neilson then referred to his own uphill fight in Shropshire and said there was no doubt that he was defeated by the plural vote. One of the first things they had down in the Liberal programme in the first session was the abolition of the plural vote, and he really believed that if they had a fair and square fight again in the division, with or without the plural vote, they would win. (Applause). Continuing Mr. Neilson addressed himself to the ' question : how was it that legislators—men who were supposed to go to the House of Commons with a full knowledge of what the people needed not only for present administration but for years to come—made Acts that in a very short time had to be wiped out? There were two reasons. Either the men who made bad Acts were ignorant or they were selfish. Touching upon what he described as the enormous and iniquitous land monopoly, Mr. Neilson said it bad come about because the men who were to-day the monopolists were the type of men who for long centuries had gone to Parliament—many of them without being sent—and had made laws so that they could have the monopoly in land. In 1801—that terrible year of "enclosures "—he found that some of the best lands in Shropshire were taken from the people and enclosed. That wag only possible because the monopolists were in power. Just a little while beforei that as much as £25,000 passed in the lobby of the House of Commons in a day buying boroughs. They talked about Tammany to-day and criticised the system i of " graft " in the United States. They talked of Rockefeller and the rest of those men. But they only learnt it from our Parliaments of the 18th century and from the legislators of that particular time. If people in New York tried to perpetrate some of the dodges that were perpetrated on our ancestors, they would be lynched. We wanted in our English system to strike first at monopoly. (Applause:) The monopolists had shifted the burden of taxation off their own shoulders and placed it on industry and the result was that industry was taxed to the extent of 95 per cent, and the landlords only 5 per cent. What the monopolists had done through Parliament, the people could undo through Parliament. (Applause.) Then, in regard to local rating, the taxation of the land upon which industry was built rather than of industry itself, would solve most of the problems that afflicted us. If they could only get the people to understand the single tax system, they need not bother about taxing incomes at all, because there was no income belonging to any particular man that bad not been brought about by labour coming into contact with land. What they wanted to do Was to get at the spurious capital—the . capital that was made by iniquitous laws—and to Itax oack the value of land to the people of England who had made the land valuable. (Applause.) What was wanted in this country was not schemes of charity but justice. (Applause.) If the land were free and labour could come in contact with It, this country could feed itself as easily as ever it did. The greatest movements of reform, they must bear in mind, had come about through peace and not through bloodshed and revolution. It was no use having a i Government with a majority ot 200 unless propaganda work went on in the constituencies. When the people made up their minds that privilese and monopoly must end, then they would see how the monopolists would fight. Away with monopoly, concluded Mr. Neilson, and let us have laws amended and new laws made, so that labour can come thoroughly in contact with the land and every man can see that he always gets the full value of the product that he makes. (Applause.) Mr. JOSEPH HOWES then addressed the meeting. They were, he said, Radicals, and they did not want to sail under any false colours. They were satisfied with their title and wanted no other. Radicalism took in all the Labour questions and a lot of other things besides, and he would be silly to limit himself to the few when I he could have all the lot. (Laughter.) The cause of humanity was the same all the world over, and a Radical should not only be a local, national and Imperial politician but a cosmopolitan politican also. With I regard to the land question, it was no use asking what the Government were going to do until they knew what the people were declaring res- pecting these matters, and how far they meant to support the Government. No Government could go in advance of the people. The land question was the bottom question in politics. Natural revenue was national revenue, and what belonged to the nation belonged to the people. Since the• Liberal Government was returned, hn immense amount of work had been done. The work which a Liberal Govertonent always had to do was the undoing of the mischief which their predecessors had created. The mischief created by the late administra- tion was done during a period of about ten years, and yet the Tories were blaming the Liberal government for not having removed it in ten months. (Laughter). It was a great tribute to the efficacy of the Liberal party that the Tories should think it possible for them to sweep the decks clean in such a very short time. At the time of the election the burning question was the question of South Africa and they were very glad to put out) bill—which some of the Tories had been very angry about—declaring that Sir Henry Camp-. bell-Bannerman had stopped the further importation of Chinese. He did it, and that was a good thing. (Applause). Just before the Tories wept out of office they issued about 16,000 licences to mine owners to bring in these Chinese. As a result of the protracted negotiations which had been going on, the mine-owners had to abandon the remaining licences and no more Chinese had to come in. (Applause.) The Government had also made a scheme for the return of some of the criminal class of Chinese but the greatest work they had done in connection with the question was the recent enforcement of the law under which the Chinese were imported, against the mineowners. Con- tinuing, Mr. Howes said the Government hadestablished self-government in South Africa and they were deter- mined not tb allow the resources of England to be used to prop up the mine-owning monopoly. Things were likely to hum out there in the immediate future and it would be well for all true reformers to assist the Government in every right move they made in the direc- tion of clearing the labour market for the workers in South Africa. (Applause.) The days of Kruger were much better days for the British workers than the days of the mining magnates since Kruger. When their Tory friends talked of Majubs and tried to throw the whole blame of the South African complication on that incident, they could be dealt with inn very summary manner. The Majuba policy took place nearly thirty years ago. It Aid not lead to war but it resulted in peace, plenty of work, prosperity and high wages in South Africa. Those conditions obtained for twenty years. Then the Tory , Government came in and reversed the Majuba policy and that led to war, the destruction of prosperity, the driving away of white labour, the introduction of Chinese, and the saddling of the people with an enormous debt. Referring to the work of the new Government, Mr. Howes said they had abolished coercion in Ireland. (Applause.) The great Bill for this Session, of course, was the Education Bill and the 1 great Bill for next Session would probably be a I Licensing Bill. Much useful administrative work had been accomplished. In Ireland grants had been msde to tenants of from .£l2O to .£131) out of the national exchequer, to help them to restock their farms on which they had been re-instated. For very many years money had been advanced to landlords from the aational exchequer, and especially had that been so in Ireland. The Liberal Government bad recognized what , some land reformers had been preaching for years—that of the two persons, the tenant and the landlord, a, security to the State for borrowed money the tenant was always a much better security than the landowner. `rl know the robbery of the jewels is bad. but there's much worse than that."

" The Russian Government cryptogram." Sir William Harding had been seated care- lessly on the arm of a lounge. He stood up now and faced Lord de Morley. It would have been hard to tell which man was the paler or the more shaken. " Gone—stolen ? " cried the younger man. "Aye, stolen," answered de Morley. "Nonsense," said Sir William Harding. " A paper like that never gets destroyed ; it turns up to destroy one. Good Heavens, de Morley, how was it lost ? " "I placed it in my bank," answered the elder man," with some other things. I would not keep it in the house as I was going away for a few days. You know the safest place in the world to place valuables is at a bank. It was in a bar, with the jewels. Well, yester- day I returned and telephoned to them to send .me the bag by special messenger ; they did sc.' and the messenger was robbed." _

He then gave- Sir William Harding an ac- count of the, whole affair.

Sir William listened somewhat impatiently. When Lord de Morley had finished he said: " The fact 'remains the document is lost. I entrusted it to you---oh I good Heavens, man, how could you have been so foolish as to have let it out of your sight even for a moment. The memorandum of a secret treaty, and such a treaty I Why, it w2ll be for me political ruination. I may, and you may, be im- peached. I don't say it mayn't even be war if that thing is not found, and found promptly." He sat down in an armchair and stared out over Carlton House Gardens with a far,s'ighted look that seemed to embrace the future and to behold his own political ruin..

It was, indeed, a terrible occurrence by which this document had been cast upon. the world to be picked up and perhaps deciphered. It was only a little piece of parchment covered with hieroglyphics, yet that little piece of parchment contained the draft of an agree- ment between two nations, an agreement the knowledge of which in certain quarters would be sufficient to cast Europe into war and ruin the party to which • Lord de Morley and Sir William Harding belonged. As the two men sat plungell in gloomy meditation the door opened and a waiter ap- peared with a card on a tray. He handed it to Lord de Morley. "J. Marcos," said that gentleman, " I wonder who he can be. Is he waiting ?"- Yes, your lordship, in the hall." " I'll /come," said his lordship, rising and little imagining that J. Mamas had been in the Guildhall Police Court that morning and had narrowly watched him as he gave his evidence before the magistrate.

CHAPTER XVII.

THE huge hall of the Reform Club presented an animated scene. Members were convers- ing in groups, members were receiving friends and passing from the coffee-room to the library, or to tthe smoking-room. Half an hour before the place had been deserted, but now it was a buzz with voices.

Lord de Morley followed the waiter who led him across the hall to where a shabby, Jewish looking man was standing with his back to the crowded hall and evidently engaged in ex- amining a large bust of Lord Palmerston.

''! Yo-u sent for me, I believe," said de 'Wor- ley, speaking in a haughty tone of voice and staring at the person before him as if wonder- ing who he could be and what his business was. . .

" Ah, yes. Excuse me," said the other turning suddenly from hi inspection of the bust, ' you are Lord de Morley 1 believe.!'

" That is ray name ? " replied de Morley. " And my name is Marcas. J. Marcas, at your service, my lord," replied the other. " I called to see if I could be of any service to "Indeed," said his lordship dryly, thinking he had to do with some shady political agent. "In what way ? I must ask you to be quick/ as I have an appointment." " In this way, my lord," said the other. "You have been robbed."

Yes, I suppose it is in the evening papers by this. I have been robbed of some jewel- lery. Well? " ._

% 4 And of some papers," said Mr. Marcas, staring full at the man opposite to him: Lord de Morley started. " Yes, and of some papers," he said. " What then ? " " Valuable papers? " said Mr. Marcas.

" The paper's 'had some value," replied Lord ~ de Morley. •• _

"The 'papers were of very great value, if I am not mistaken, my lord, replied Alarms. Lord de Morley paused for a moment as if in thought, then he said: " Come outside the club and talk tone about this."

Now Mr. Marcas had nothing to go upon, but the fact that in giving his evidence at the police court Lord de Morley had betrayed nervousness, but such an astute observer of human nature was he that he felt certain that the documents lost in the robbery were not only of vital importance, but that they held some secret capable of bringing shame or • disaster should it be discovered. Great rogues, like great geniuses, work by intuition, and it may be said of Mr. Marcas that if he was not a great genius he was at all events a very great rogue. . .

He was also possessed of a most surprising stock of impudence. ' "Now,' said Lord de Morley, when they were outside, "what do you know of this business; explain yourself, please ? " " At present,". said Mr. Mamas, " I know very little, but I hope to know more before long." \ %"Was it to tell me this that you sent for me and put rue to the trouble of an interview when I was busy? " asked de Morley in icy tones.

"My lord," said Marcas, " I was once a solicitor, and I know more about the thieves of London than perhaps any other man. I have come to offer my services-" De Morley cut him short. "You have come to offer me your services as a detective I suppose ? "

7`-Precisely." " Why, confound your impudence," blazed out his lordship. "What do you mean by coming to my club and troubling me like this. 1 thought yout had some important statement to make. A detective. The affair is already in the hands of detectives. I wish you a good- day, sir."

He turned-on his heel and marched back to his club, leaving Marcas standing white with rage on the pavement. lie had intended worming himself into Lord de Morlev's confidence and then, if possible, blackmailing him, but the summary manner in which that nobleman had dealt with him put a stop to this pretty little scheme, but it caused a flame of rage in his evil heart and a determination to be . even with this con- temptuous aristocrat at all hazards. The devil rarely puts an evil design into a man's head without putting a tool close to his hand with which to accomplish that design.

As Mr. Marcas watched the retreating figure of the man who had treated him in such a con- temptuous manner he noticed on the opposite side of the way another figure. It was that of a young man well dressed in a frock coat and tall hat, wearing a camellia in his buttonhole and an eye glass in his eye. He was undistinguishable from the hundred and one clubmen one meets in a walk any day up' the Haymarket and Piccadilly, and you would never have imagined that this elegant lounger was one of the greatest rascals in London and the mpst expert theif in Europe, one of those thieves who would compel our admira- tion of their adroitness did they not fill our - souls with horror.

John Stirling was the name this person went under. Marcas had defended him success- fully sdmt; years ago at Bow Street, and the power and ability displayed by him, on that occasion had left an i , ndelible impression on the mind of his client.

" Hullo, Marcas," cried the young man as Marcos crossed the street and stopped him, "where have you sprung from " "I want to have a moment's talk with you," said Alarms. " Where shall we go? "

" The best place for conversation is the street," replied the other. " I am going to the Cafe Cavour ; we can talk as we go." "I have an idea in, my,head that niay mean thousands," said Marca.s, as they turned up the Haymarket ; " thousands if you will help me."

"What is your idea ? " "You have heard of the great robbery." " In Holland Park ? " " Yes." " Ah," said Stirling, heaving a sigh, " I have just read it Lin the evening papers. That was a coup if you like." "I know a bigger one—ao you know, can you guess, who pulled this affair off." " Who ? " " The Grafter." " The Grafter," cried Stirling. " Why I met him this morning looking as pious as if he had just come out of church." "When will you meet him again? " "Goodness knows." " You must , meet him again soon," said Marcas, suddenly stopping and confronting his companion: Do you know what the Grafter carries about with him ? Well, I'll tell you. I'd swear he has in his pocket at this moment an opal worth a king's ransom, but the opal is nothing to the papers which I am also certain he carries on his person, fol. they. are too valuable, if my suspicions are correct, for him to place them anywhere else. Stirling, we must get possession of thos‘, papers." The two fnen walked in earnest convers-- tion till they reached the Cafe Cavour. Here Marcas stopped. "You -have not lost your old facility for appropriating other people's property, I sup- pose," he asked. Stirling smiled: " Search in your pockets and see what money you have got," he answered. Marcos put his fingers into his waistcoat pocket. " Good Heavens I " he cried, robbed ! "

" I have beer.

" Just 'so," said .Stirling,- producing .half a dozen sovereigns, "I robbed you a minute O,T). No, I haven't lost my facility in.appropriattng other people's property. Here; take your money back a.nd.. come in and have a drink and talk this matter over. I like the idea. I have many an Old score to settle • with the Grafter, and this seems a chance of sett in; the • • (To be continue .)

CHAPTER XV—Continucd.

Miss Princeps was the next witness, and a very troublesome witness- she proved. An ordinary person -who knew her slightly might have fancied that in this crisis she, would have sided dgainst her niece. They would have found themselves entirely out of their reckoning. The state of her mind as she stood in the witness box was cute of wild indignation that Daisy should even be suspected of anything wrong, coupled with intense irritation against Mr. Rowlands, Lord de Morley, the alderman on the bench, and everyone who had anything to do with the affair.

She did not help. Daisy's case by her snap- pish answers, and the result of the proceed- ings was that Arthur Esmond was formally remanded for -a week and a warrant of arrest was issued for the apprehension of Miss Daisy Graham.

The alderman offered to accept bail in one thousand pounkls for 'Arthur's re-appearance, but as it was not forthcoming he was removed in.custody. Mr. Rowlands went back to the bank, and Miss Princeps drove home. When the final decision was reached Arthur felt himself touched, on the arm ; a warder was beside him who led/him out of court down a long passage, and into a little office. Here he was searched, and a penknife which he had upon him was taken away. After a few other formalities and 'some entries niade in a book he was led down a stone passage, a door was opened, he passed through the doorway, the door shut upon him with a clang, and he found himself a prisoner. It was an ordinary police cell where prisoners are placed before removal to Hollo- way.

It was about fifteen feet long by eight broad, the walls were tiled, and a wooden bench ran along one wall. There was a bell in one corner that a prisoner might ring if ill, and there was a grille in the door that a prisoner might look out through if he wanted amusement, though all the view he would have consisted of the stone wall of the passage. Arthur sat down on the bench and looked about him. The fact that he was a prioner, the fact that a warrant of arrest had been issued for Daisy, the fact that they were both innocent, these three facts 'combined caused the unfortunate young man almost to doubt the mercy of Providence.. The thought that she had run away, that she was alone in London, almost maddened him.

He was seated engaged in glooray.. medita- tion when he heard a sound ousiae. The grille of the cell door was opened for a second, a warder peeped in then the eloor itself was opened,' and a gentleman entAred. It was Lord de Morley. ' The door closed behind him. , I

, " I have come to have a moment's conver- sation with you, Mr. Esmond," said his lord- ship, taking his seat on the bench near Arthur and placing his hat beside him. "I have just had a word with the magis- trate and I have ;decided to become your bail, for I am very well convinced that you are innocent of any hand in this matter."

"My lord," said Arthur, " I am absolutely innocent, but I am thinking less of myself than of the unfortimate lady—" "Alt, you mean Miss Graham?" cut in his lordship.

" les ; she, too, is absolutely innocent." " Quite so. Your explanation of the pas- sage in her letter is pretty clear to me, but it is suspicious, and under all the circum- stances of the c et, you must not grumble at the course we have pursued. Now I wish to ask you, do you think you would recognise again the .man who has evidently robbed you ? " _

" The old man ? " Yes." "I would recognise him anywhere, but • • Yes." " I could not swear that he iobbed me. I lost consciousness." " It was he who did it ? " said Lord de Morley inn positive tone. "He was with you last. Even if you fainted and your uncon- sciousness was not due to any diabolical art of his, he, took the bag. Mr. Esmond, there were papers in that bag that must be recovered." And jewels ? "I am not thinking of the jewels. I would give the lot to-morrow for the papers in the bag. Those papers must be recovered. You are the only person who has seen the thief, and instead of keep:ng you boxed up in gaol where you can hoof no use:l have determined to act as your bail so that you may go free. I only ask you in return to keep your eyes open, go about as much as you can, and try, if possible, to find this man. I know the chances are in- finitely small, but still there is a chance that you' may run against him. You will also be free if I want you at any time to identify any- one. _Now if you Will come with me I will go with you agt..f.n before the magistrate and offer my bail."

CHAPTER XVI. HALF an hour later Arthur Esmond was standing in the street a free man. Free, that is to say, for the moment. He had given Lord' de Morley his address with a promise that he would do all that lay in his power to aid in the search for the thief. It seemed a hopeless quest in the maze of Lon- don, doubly hopeless as he stood on the pave- ment watcVing the unceasing stream of people flowing past.

• Meanw' hile Lord de Morley, having gone through all the official formaliti,zs necessitated by the police in a case of bail, called a hansom and drove westward. At •2haring Cross he dismissed the cab and took his way on foot, to the Reform Club.

It was the luncheon hour and the great hall, or the "saloon," as it is Called by the mem- bers of the club, was empty. _ . .

Lord de Morley crossed it hurriedly and made for the little smoking-room on the first floor.

He rang and a club attendant answered the summons.

" Is Sir William Harding in the club? " he asked.

"Sir William is at luncheon in the large coffee-room, your lordship," answered the man.

"Oh, give him this." , Lord de Morley scribbled a couple of words on a visiting card and handed it to the waiter. Then he sat down, lit a cigar, and contem- plated the trees of Carlton House Gardens amidAt whose branches the summer wind was lazily blowing. Scarcely five minutes had elapsed when the door opened and a young man entered the MOM.

He was an extremely handsome' man of thirty-five or so, well groomed, well dressed, and evidently filled with health and good spirits. At this momettt, indeed, many a man might • have envied Sir William Harding. Rich. young, handsome, a member of the Govern- ment, and engaged to be married to Estelle de Morley, Lord de Morley's only daughter. It was easy to predict a splendid career for him. " Well, de Morley," he said in his careless, easy going manner, "what's up? I had only just finished lunchor Why, man, you look pale—liver again ? "

Sit down like a good fellow," answered de Morley. "You have heard nothing of the robbery, have you ? "

"Robbery :what robbery ? " "A bag of mine has bean stolen.'l " When ? " " Yesterda3s." " Were there any valuables in it ? "About forty thousand pounds' worth od iewels." answered Lord de Alorlevd - •

ARRINGTON EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

ally be would have prepared quarterly returns—which would form permanent records for future reference should a report made of an unsatisfactory character. Personally he lihd always advocated a liberal scale of payments for teachers but he had always insisted that for every spent they should get a pounds' worth of value, and instead of the regulations being relaxed they ought, in his opinion, to be even more strict. If his amendment were approved he hoped that the School Management Committee would go very carefully into the question and devise something which would be satisfactory to the committee, but he strongly objected to the original minute being varied without an alternative being suggested. There seemed to be an opinion that the increments shonld'be automatic. He said most emphatically that those increments should be regarded as a premium on good work and should be given in recognition for services. He knew many teachers and it gave him] pleasure to boar testi- mony to the admirable services as a whole given by them but they had to make regulations not for the best teacher but for th,ose who happened to fall short of the high standard. Mr. ROBERTS seconded the amendment. The teachers had a misconception of the purport and intention of the original resolution. When the matter was discussed and that resolution was agreed upon it was felt that would be unfair, where the report of ahead teacher con- tained a reflection on any meruher of his staff, that that report should be submitted without giving the person so reported upon an opportunity of answering, before the Committee, any 'charges made against him. With, that safeguard be failed to see anything in the resolu- tion which was contrary to business principles or to which any exception could be taken. He knew several firms in Warrington in which periodical reports were received from the heads of the departments. The resolution which the sub-committee submitted that evening was far more invidious than before because the head teacher was in the position of not having to report on good work but only to report upon those members of his staff who were not doing their duty. Where a headmaster had only to report upon the misconduct a slackness'of any member of his staff and knew that that report would probably result, nay,almost certainly result in the refusal to increase the salary of the person reported upon he would think a good many times before he made it. If he had as a matter of ordinary business to make a report upon every member of his staff it would not be so invidious. Mr. PLINSTON supported tie minutes. He did not see why they should put so much extra work on the head teachers as to require half yearly reports. He did not think the head teachers would neglect their duty by not sending in reports as suggested in the minutes when necessar Councillor PEMBERTON also supported the minutes. They must take some notice of the wishes of the teachers themselves. They might as well call a spade a spade and what they wanted to get at was anything which was unsatisfactory. The resolution met the wishes of the teachers and if it would secure that they had efficiency he thought they should adopt it. Councillor LORD also supported the minutes. Mr. C. E. Parker said he would like to know what objection the teachers had to being commended. (Hear, hear.) He thought it desirable that the Committee should have a knowledge of the good as well as the bad points of the teachers. (Hear, hear.) Councillor PEMBERTON : The head teachers do net I want the work of having to report twice a year. Mr. llPsos said he could assure the committee that the idle teacher who shirked his work would meet with less mercy from the teachers themselves than from any other body. They were jealously concerned foi the good name of their profession and were eager to expel from their ranks those who brought discredit upon them by shirking or by going about their work in an inefficient way. Personally he objected altogether to reports. If they ran a business they had a man- in charge at an adequate salary and said " Now produce good work " and trusted him. Why need they be suspicious of him and want a report every six months on the members of his staff ? Mr. JOLLEY : Because he does not pay their salaries.. Mr. UPSON continuing said the head was interested in , seeing that he got good work. His own professional reputation was at stake. He did not think it was a universal practice in business to expect periodical reports. The Town Clerk and the Borough Treasurer did not report to the Council unless a man failed in his duty. The difficulty they had in getting young men to I take up teaching lay in the fact that they made the ' conditions so irksome and the life so irritating —(Mr. Roberts: By this? Nonsense)—Speaking as one who knew more than probably any member of the committee about the teaching profession he maintained that the want of recruits for the profession 'Was due, not to the I comparative want of prospects but to the irksomeness I and irritation caused by the restrictive regulations which the committee imposed upon the teachers. The I teachers did not object to reports. They only objected to secret reports. The resolution was a very fair com- promise and commended itself to a large extent to the teachers in the employ of the committee. The Rev. Canon STEVENSON, in reply, said their position was this—that they felt it was their duty to ascertain that the assistant teachers were discharging their duties efficiently before they recommended them the Finance Committee for an increase of salary. The gentlemen who formed the deputation expressed the unanimous opinion of the teachers that they preferred I the method adopted in the resolution. Teachers who I had been assistants for many years, and had given long and honourable service, did not like being reported upon every six months. If a teacher was not doing his work lit was the duty of the headmaster to make an adverse I report. ' After some further discussion the amendment was put to the meeting and carried by ten votes to nine. GLYMPSES OF SEA AND MOUNTAIN IN WALES. - .

The Cambrian Railways Co. issued a charmin Illustrated booklet with the above title. It is briefly and brightly descriptive of some of the most beautiful Rrts of North and South Wales. Though it seems rather late in the season to be talking of holidays, yet many of the watering places on the Cardigan Bay coast- line are particularly riviting in the autumn. At any rate the booklet, with its beautiful illustrations and good reading, together with information about golf- links, coaching tours and the like should be welcomed by every lover of wild Wales whether bent on going there immediately or not. A postcard to Mr. C. S. Dennis, general manager of the Cambrian Railways, Oawestry will procure a copy of the booklet.

The Examiner, Saturday, September 22, 1906. SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS. NATIONAL SERVICE LEAGUE.

NEW BRITISH' FISHES.

The as. Helga has been investigating the seas off the coast of Ireland since 1901. The results have been published in "The Fisheries of Ire- land Scientific) Investigations." From this we !tarn that twenty-eight new species of fish have been added US the British list. One of these, blelamphaes euryleßis, is said to be new to science.

(Applause.) He moved a vote of thanks to the speakers. _ .

Mr. H. Robehs thought the signs of the times were encouragingly in the direction of the adoption of the principles of • the National SerVice League, when they , saw the principal Liberal newsplper of the country giving expression to sentiments which were in accord with those principles. He noticed that the " Manchester Guardian " said It is a sacred obligation on every citizen to defend his home from invasion."—lt was the cardinal principle of the League, to have a nation trained to arms. He would suggest a motto for the League taken from the Bible, in addition to the one which they had, which was " LA us play the man for our people and for the cities of our God." (Applause.) Colonel Pilkington said he would like to ask a ques- tion "Why should so few serve and so many not ?" Let every man learn to serve his country and let them see that our great navy upon which the empire depended was kept up to the old standard—as big and strong as the combined navies of any two powers. With regard to the statement that they preached wite he would like to ask who was the most likely to be opposed to war the man who paid men to fight for him or the man who knew that in the event of war he would have to give up his business and go to fight. He thought the latter was of the two the least likely to rush into war. (Applause.) In conclusion Canon Willis spoke in high terms of the great enthusiasm which Captain Crosfield displayed in furthering the movement. Mr.J.H.Smethurst seconded and the vote was heartily accorded.

A brief reply by Lord Newton brought the speech- making to a close.

===== 0002601_19060922_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
MOTOR-CARS AND SORE THROATS.

Diseases among children—notably sore throat and bad eyes—caused through dust raised by motor-cars, is most rife where the schools are situated on roads frequented by motor-cars, or where the children traverse these roads. In one school the head-teacher points out they have ten cases of " sore, throat" where five years ago they had one.

SOUND SIGNAL AS MINE EXPLODER.

A curious device for exploding mines at some distance is operated by waves of sound. In the , interior of a cylindrical resonator a disc is so mounted as to be free to turn about its diameter. When a certain note is sounded, this disc will place itself in a plane perpendicular to the cylin- der. By causing the turning disc to complete an electric circuit, a mine can be exploded by means of a signal given by a warship's siren tuned to the same note as the resonator.

===== 0002601_19060922_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
A NOVEL ALLOY.

A novel alloy, shewn by Mr. J. E. Stead at a soirée of the London Royal Society, offers strik- ing possibilities 'for ornamental effects.. It. is composed of 75 per cent, of tin, 20 of antimony, and 5 of arsenic, and on slow fusion it deposits semi-spherical shell-like crystals in an ordinary metal matrix. Dilute acids dissolve away part of the matrix, leaving in relief the crystals, which resist the acid on account of their comparative richness in antimony and arsenic. The spherical patterns take a high polish, and may be given greater prominence by running wax into the cavities.

===== 0002601_19060922_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
A SIGNAL LIGHT FOR LIFEDUOYS.

A marine torch or signal light that bursts into a brilliant flame the instant it touches water is a useful invention. These signal lights can be used in many ways. One method is to attach one to a lifebuoy; the moment the life preserver strikes the water the light gives out a bright flame, shewing its location not only to the person over- board, but marking the spot for the crew, who put back in a small boat, says Popular Mechanics. As the average passenger steamer cannot be stopped in much less than- a mile when under full headway, the importance of the light in finding the spot is evident. The signal burns with 300-candle-power for a full hour. These lights are in use in the American Navy.

===== 0002601_19060922_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
PAPER FROM COTTON STALKS.

The manufacture of paper from the fibre of the cotton stalk is one bf the latest inventions which are said to have passed the experimental stage. It is asserted, says the World's Work, that all grades of paper, from the best form of linen to the lowest 'grade, can be manufactured from cot- ton stalks. In addition to this, a variety of by- products, such as alcohol, nitrogen, material for guncotton and smokeless powder, can also be se- cured in paying quantities. Mills for the use of cotton stalks in that way may become general in the cotton-growing States. It is estimated that on an area of land producing a bale of cotton, at least one ton of stalks can be gathered. Upon this basis, from 10,000,000 to 12,000,000 tons of raw material could be secured for the production of paper, which would increase the value of the cotton crop of the Southern States nearly £2,000,000.

===== 0002601_19060922_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
LONG LIFE AND ITS SECRET.

There can be little doubt, says Science Siftings, that life is shortened by our notions concerning the natural duration of life. Everybody has come to think that when a man is seventy years old he is very old; when he is eighty years old he had better die; and if he lives until he is ninety years old he is a curiosity fit for a museum. These notions alone are sufficient to kill the average person. Whatever a large multitude of people continually think and say will practically come to be the truth. This talk of old age and dying when a man is seventy, eighty, or ninety years old is entirely unnecessary. Men and women ought to live until they are 100 years old, and be- yond. When we get this thoroughly in our minds, talk as if it were true, act as if it were true, we will do more towards lengthening the span of human life than in any other way. "As a, man thinketh, so is he." If we keep ori think- ing and saying that we are old and ready to die, then we will get old and get ready to die.

===== 0002601_19060922_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
EARTHQUAKE RECORDS.

horizontal eo.rTrvihjzae o.t ostnat lr uo instrumentfttp,eeearthquakes t.uhß generallylq.uus-ma„k eiistsh known, rr employediluns ae h sn t hteii,ie b inlnooen, . The apparatus is very simple in principle. Most readers will have noticed how the boom df a cutter at anchor swings violently from side to side if it is not se- cured, even though the motion of the boat may be very slight. It is on this principle that the seismometer, or earthquake measurer, is con- structed. A vertical rod, rising from- a firm foun- dation, corresponds to the cutter's mast, and car- ries a horizontal rod, corresponding to the boom. The latter is free to swing to one side or itho other, if the mast is tilted even to a very slight extent, by an earthquake movement. The extent of these lateral swings measures the intensity. of the earthquake shock. This is the whole principle of the instrument. It is, however, necessary to have some automatic method of recording its in- dications continuously; for it is impossible to tell beforehand when an earthquake may come. The recording arrangement is photographic. The end of the boom carries an opaque horizontal metal screen, with an aperture in it. Underneath a Strip of photographic film is caused to travel slowly past, by means of clockwork. Light from a, lamp passes through the aperture and im- presses a spot on the photographic film. If all remains steady, this spot of light is drawn out into a uniform straight line by. the motion of the film. If, however, the boom is caused to swing Ifrom side to side by an earthquake movement, the spot of light necessarily moves in a similar I manner, and the narrow line before recorded is ' locally broadened out. When the movement sub- sides, the photographic trace becomes larrow again. Thus an examination of the record shows exactly when, and to what extent, the instrument has been shaken by an earthquake shock.

ANNUAL MEETING OF THE WARRINGTON BRANCH.

Lord Newton and the Secretary for War.

The annual meeting of the Warrington Branch of the National Service League was held at Appleton Hall, the residence of the President, Mr. T. H. Lyon, on Saturday, when the proceedings were of an enjoy- able character. The extensive and well-laid-out grounds attached to the Hall were thrown open for inspection, and after the business had been concluded and refreshments partaken of, many spent a pleasant hour in these beautiful surroundings. Messrs. Cros- field's band also in attendance and an added pleasure was afforded by their capable contribution of a number of selections. Lord Newton presided over a large attendance, and and amongst those present were the Duchess of Buckinghain and Cbandos, Viscount Cross, G.C.8., Miss D. Cross, Lord Raglan, the Hon. J. and Mrs. Cross, Col. and the Hon. G. Egerten Warburton, Mr. Peers Egerton Warburton, Lady Brooke, Mrs. Crowley, Lady Greenell, Sir Charles Bexall, K.C.13., Mr. and Mrs. Leicester Warren, Mrs. Langford Brooke, Mr. and Mrs. Vasher, Major and Mrs. Molyneux Teale, Col. Pilkington, Leint. Chamberlain, R.N. Mr. A. H. Crosfleld, M.P. the Rev. Canon Willis and Captain Croseeld (hon. sec.) Lord Egerton, who should have taken the chair, wrote :—I much regret that I shall be prevented by recent indisposition from attending the meeting on Saturday, as I hoped to have been able to do. I trust that the Socialistic views of the conference of the trade unions will not be allowed to counteract the patriotic efforts of Earl Roberts to impress on every able-bodied citizen the duty of qualifying himself by some training in the use of the rifle for the defence of his country. Lord Newton expressed his pleasure at seeing so large a gathering. He only regretted that they did not see the activity which characterized the Warrington Branch, manifest in the other Branches of tile League throughout the country. He expressed his gratification at the presence of Lord Raglan whose duty it was a few years ago, as the official representative in the House of Lords of the War office to defend the voluntary system which was no longer adapted to the needs of the country. He regarded Lord Raglan's conversion to the views of the National Service League with more pleasure than he was able to express ; it would be most pleasurable of all if he was able to see signs of repent- ance on the part of former and present Ministers. So far as he,could see there appeared to be little sign of change upon the part of the latter. Both past and present Ministers seemed to be engaged in the femiliar and congenial task of throwing dust in the eyes of the British public and in endeavouring to make them believe in shams instead of realities. Speaking as one who had never been a minister,and therefore impartially he had never seen any great difference between the representatives of the various governments, at all evea ts, with regard to the question of national defence. The only difference was as to degree and the only question was as to which was the worst of the lot. The person, however, who was more worthy of blame than any of his predecessors was the present War Minister, although he (the speaker) observed signs of grace even in him. Mr. Haldane was exceptionally dangerous, because he had, unfortuately, the reputation of being a moderate man, and when a moderate man had as his colleagues persons to whom one would hardly apply that epithet, it was difficult to attack him, because he could always be defended upon the score of moderation. Anyone who took even the most frivolous interest in the qnestion must see that we bad arrived at a highly critical position. We had arrived at a period when the question of national defence was considered, not with reference to the requirements of the nation, but solely with regard to financial questions. In other words, the question of national defence was to be determined, not by the needs of the country, but by the amount of taxation which they could get out of the country without rendering the Government unpopular. He did not think it possible to arrive at a more thoroughly unsatisfactory state of things than that. If anything could be more unsatis- factory it was the arguments which were used in the defence of the reduction which was now going on of our naval and military forces. fie trusted he was not too hopeful in anticipating some benefit from what he read in the papers that day. Even Mr. Haldane, in a speech on Friday, delivered sentiments which might with absolute pro- priety be uttered from that very platform. Mr. Haldane stated that what we required above all things was great expansion in war, and in order to obtain this expansion they must go to the nation for co-operation that the only safeguard for this country was a nation; in arms ; that the obligation of national defence and of the individual being made to stand up for his hearth and home was not militarism ;and until we had an army based upon the people that army could not possibly compare favourably with any other. The only differ- ence that arose between that League and Mr. Haldane was how that programme was to be carried out. What the League said was that the voluntary system was no longer capable of affording us the kind of force we required. The system had proved itself unequal to our necessities and had completely broken down. Surely the people by this time had grasped the fact that they could not get what was needed unless they adopted the principle of compulsion; and in the adoption of this principle lay the only solution of our defects. If responsible men could be found with sufficient courage to urge this upon their countrymen, and if a Government could be found bold enough to take the matter up be ventured to predict not only would their scheme be easy to carry out, but that it would render us practically invulnerable, would bestow upon us the blessing of peace, and be productive of many and untold advantages, not only to this generation, but to those fated to follow us. (Applause). Lord Raglan, in moving the adoption of the annual report, said he had come to the conclusion that the voluntary system had broken down but immediately they talked to the people about military service the cry was raised : " Conscription ;we will not have it." In the first place the National Service League did not advocate conscription. They advocated universal service—that everyone should take a turn at doing what was everyone's duty. They were always told that the country would never stand compulsion but as nobody had ever asked the country, certainly no general election had ever been fought on the question,nobody had a right to say the country would refuse, and he did not believe the country would refuse. The difficulty of the present system was recruiting and army reform had always been stultified by the question of recruiting. He commanded one of the strongest militia corps in the country, and he found the first, second and third difficulties,were recruiting. We had an lEmpire to defend and we never had had, and under the present system never could get what was essential—a proper sense of security, and that was what the National Service League wanted to give. The question was "Was the country going to act before disaster came or after ?" Lord Raglan proceeded to show that Title clubs were not sufficient. What was wanted was a thorough and progressive system of instruction which the proposals of the League would give. FINE WIRE DRAWING. (Applause.) , The common spider continues to hold firet. It was silken, flossy strand so fine that 18,000 of them urged against the League that they preached war. If no more than equal in thickness an ordinary he had learnt one thing during his travels It was the thread of sewing silk. But in the world of ma- horror of war, but if he had learnt another it was that chinery and industrial art, says a writer in the nine times out of ten the country which was beaten New -York Tribune, some wonderful achieve- deserved to be beaten. THE METHODICAL HOUSEWIFE.

You can always tell a methodical woman by the com- fort of her home. She is the envy of her sex—loved and admired by husband, children, friends, and neigh- bours.

How easily she seems to go through life ! Her house is always epic and span, things never have to be hunted for, and she seems to be always as neat and clean as everything around her, Work seems to get done for the asking, and it isn't half such a burden upon her as it is upon less methodical women, who have to scrub and dust and sew from morn till night. There's never a screw loose in the domestic machinery of the methodical housewife, and in one particular especially she invariably displays her wisdom and fore- thought. Upon a handy shelf in the living room you will find a box and a bandage, with perhaps a piece of lint and a pair of scissors. The box contains her supply of Zam-buk, and she is so careful to have it there because she has learnt by experience that there is nothing half so good for cuts, bruises, burns, scalds, scrapes, chapped hands, eruptions, rashes, and every injured or irritated condition of the skin. Scarcely a week passes without the box of Zama-link coming in useful for one or other of these troubles. It "pays for its shelf-room " day after day, and all members of the family are constantly moved to remark upon the indispensability of this wholesome healer, and upon the forethought of the housewife who provided them with it., Zam-buk saves no end of pain and trouble for them all. As a first-aid it is quite excellent because it com- bines soothing, healing, and antiseptic qualities. The mischievous youngsters' scrapes and bruises, father's cuts, the scalds of a washing day, burns sustained while cookiog or ironing, dusting-knocks, sprains, and all such injuries are healed up cleanly and rapidly by judicious dressings with Zam-buk, and it prevents festering poisoning, or disease. Mother's hands are chapped and sore—Zam-buk restores them to softness and strength ; her feet are tired and tender with over- work—Zam-buk gives ease and soundness. Baby's skin is chafed and hot, or inclined to irritate and erupt in nasty rashes—Zam-buk cools, refreshes, and clears. The pretty daughter's face is disfigured by pimples—Zam- Buk makes the skin healthy and beautiful again. And so the list of daily benefits might be prolonged indefinitely. It is not to be wondered at, then, that the methodical housewife never allows herself to be without Zam-buk. You will do well to take a leaf out of her book, and keep a box handy in your home.
