WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

DEPUTY MEDICAL OFFICER APPOINTED.

Procedure Questioned.

A meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday morning, under the presidency of Mr. J. Billing°. There were also present :—Mrs. Wilkinson, Rev. T. R. Matthews, Dr. .7% lurray, Messrs. W. Hickman, A. J. Jolley, S. Davies, R. Forshaw, J. Payton, R. Bolton, T. H. Ashcroft. E. L. Bent, G. Thompson, A. Brooks, W. Houghton,G.Mather, W. C. Brooks. W. Flannagan, J. Cook, M. W. Seton, J.C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (assistant clerk), J. F.Acvbery (master), and A. Wright (superintendent of Padgate schools).

===== 0002601_19060922_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
COST OF DEPUTATIONS.

Mr. S. Davies in moving the passing of cheques for the day which included the expenses of deputations, said he wished to utter a Word of caution. The number of deputations which were taking place warranted special attention being called to them. PADGATE SCEIOOLS.

At a meeting of the Padgate Schools Committee the chairman (Mr. Mather) reported that he and Mrs. Clay as a sub-committee had gone into the question of the internal painting of the homes and it was resolved that the sub-committee be empowered to engage suitable men to do the work, to purchase materials, and arrange for a sitting room. The following tenders were accepted for the half year :—Milk (for a year), J. W. Houghton; fuel, Messrs. Pendlebury and Co. ; meat, Mr. Baldwin ; drapery and tailors' goods, T. J. Lee; bread and flour. J. Hallwood ; brushes, S. N. Green ; groceries, Messrs. H. Milling and Sons. The minutes were approved. HOUSE COMMITTEE'S BUSINESS.

At a meeting of the House Committee on September 7th, the following tenders were accepted for the ensuing half year :—Fish and ice, W. Whittaker and Son ; meat, Henry Singleton ; groceries, Henry Miffing and Co. : milk, Whitlow and Derbyshire ; wines and spirits, R. Rowson and Sons ; flour and oatmeal, James Hal'wood ; brooms and brushes, Sam N. Green ; gloves, William Jones; coffins, A. E. Mason : coal, Pendlebury and Co.; drapery and tailor's goods, T. J. Lee ; boots and shoes, Alfred Taylor. .

A comniunication was read from the Town Clerk con- taining a suggestion from the Fire Engine Committee that the Guardians should extend their water mains to the front of the nurses' homes and fix a double hydrant. The committee after carefully considering the matter and inspecting existing facilities, concluded that the proposed extension was not necessary and the clerk was directed to reply to that effect. The minutes were approved. THE RATES.

The CLERK submitted the usual detailed statement on the estimates of expenditure during the half-year ending Lady Day, 1907. This showed that a call of sixpence in the g upon the ratable values of each Union township would be required. Mr. JOLLEY moved and Mr. HICKMAN seconded that the estimates be approved, and a rate of 6d. in the £ made. The CHAIRMAN': The small call of Gd. in the £ should commend itself to all the ratepayers. The motion was agreed to. THE MAINTENANCE OF PAUPER LUNATICS.

A letter was read from Mr. Harcourt E. Clare, Clerk to the Lancashire Asylums Board, inviting the Chair- man of the Board, the Chairman of the House Com- mittee, and the Clerk to a special conference of the Lancashire Asylums Board to be held at Preston on the 27th inst. The object of the Conference was to discuss how far it might be possible by a closer 00-operation between the Asylums Board and the Boards of Guardians in the county to reduce the cost of the maintenance of pauper lunatics to the ratepayers. At the present time, roughly speaking, three-fourths of the certified lunatics were maintained in the asylums, and one-fourth in the Workhouses. - -

"It was that the chairman of the Board, the chairman of the House Committee,and the Clerk should attend the Conference. THE COST OF VACCINATION. "AN ABOMINABLE STATE OF THINGS."

In response to a request of Mr. William Houghton the Clerk submitted a statement as to the cost V vaocination. The following are the details :—Fees paid to public vaccinators during four years ended Lady Day 1899 :—Lady Day 1896, £134 Ig. 6d.-1807, £l3l 145.- 1898, £l2B Os. 10d.-1899, £134 M.—Total £528 13s. td. Average £132 3s. Bd. Same for four years ended Lady Day, 1903 : —Lady Day 1900,1447 195.-1901, £515 175.- 1902, £621 Os. 63.-1903, £1063 6s. Bd.—Total Z 2648 12s. average £662 31. Same for the last three years :—Lady Day 1904, £1157-1905,2900 18s.-1906, £732 13s.—Total £2790 11s. Average £O3O 3s. Bd. Mr. HouoirroN said he would not move a resolution but be wanted to call attention to the abominable state 1 of things which had been in vogu• during the past

seven or eight years. His remarks were modified owing to the information which the clerk had given him that the Local Government Board were about to issue a new order dealing with the vaccination question. At the same time he did not think there was any harm in keep- ing the sore open until the matter was rectified. An average of £930 for a population like they had in the Warrington Union was abnormal and abominable. The CLERK said that according to the estimates the amount for the next half-year would be even larger. Mr. HOUGHTON : There is no greater advocate of vaccination than myself, but I don't think we ought to pay this price for it. I suppose we are paying on the minimum scale too. The CL'ERK : That is so. I believe the Local Govern- ment Board have already prepared a revised scale of payments favourable to Poor Law Unions and it: will receive confirmation when Parliament meets.

The CHAIRMAN said the3,--Ve-re—ir;TleTted to Mr. Houghton for bringing the matter forward. THE ASSESSMENT COMMITTEE.

The CLERK said the Assessment Committee had lost the services of Mr. William Owen as valuer and had decided to appoint a successor at the same salary—£2oo. Authority was given to the committee to proceed with the appointment. Mr. THOMPSON : As an overseer I may say we are very glad to hear that a valuer is to be appointed as we think it is very necessary. DEPUTY MEDICAL OFFICER. WARM DISCUSSION.

The special committee which has dealt with the Medical Officer question, having considered the appli- cations for the post of Deputy Workhouse Medical Officer recommended that Dr. G. Harold Joseph be appointed to perform the duties of Workhouse Medical Officer during the annual holidays of the Resident Medical Officer, to act for him during any temporary absence or in case of illness and to assift him when necessary at surgical operations; that the remuneration to Dr. Joseph for his services be at the rate of £35 a year and that the engagement be determinable by one month's notice on either side. The CHAIRMAN moved and Mr. JOLLEY seconded the adoption of the recommendation. Mr. ASHCROFT asked who authorised the sub-com- mittee to advertise for a Deputy Medical Officer, whether the matter had been before the House Com- mittee,and if so on what date. He also asked for the names of the applicants for the post and the names of the Guardians present at the special committee meeting. The CHAIRMAN said the Board appointed the special committee on August Bth and left it with that com- mittee to make all necessary arrangements as to the introduction of the resident medical officer.

Mr. ASHCROFT : But a deputy medical officer was never mentioned at all.

The CHAIRMAN : You can't do without deputy. Mr. ASFICROFT : The matter should have been brought before the House Committee. Three or four men have no right to dictate to the whole Board. We are all Guardians. The CHAIRMAN: Pardon me—

Mr. ASHCROFT : lam right. The CHAIRMAN : The Board appoints a special com- mittee with power to act. 1 shall rule you out of order now. Don't you introduce such statements here. Mr. Asticnorr : -I still say a deputy medical officer was not mentioned. If lam wrong I will give way. The CLERK then read the conditions sanctioned by the Board on August 3rd with regard to the appoint- ment of the resident medical officer. These included the payment of a deputy during the holidays of the resident doctor and the provision of such professional assistance as might be needed at operations. Mr. ASHCROFT said there was no statement that a permanent official should be appointed. It was not fair that the matter should have been dealt with, and the House Committee not have an opportunity of dis- cussing it. Mr. DAVIES said he must confess that he was sur- prised when he received the intimation of the appoint- ment of a deputy, but if he was to understand that the appointment formed part of the work of the special committee it somewhat lessened his surprise. He thought at first that the committee had exceeded their duty, and it would have been better if they had waited until the resident medical officer had got into harnesS and then have consulted with him and the House Com- mittee with reference to the additional appointment. Mr. THOMPSON also expressed surprise that the Com- mittee had made the appointment. In his view, they required a consultant to assist in severe cases, and he thought the question should be referred to the House Committee. The matter had been hurried too much, some members.of the committee being away on their holidays. In answer to Mr. THOMPSON, the CHAIRMAN said the other applicants for the position were Dr. Howard and Dr. Binns, both of Stockton Heath, but the committee considered the fact that they lived outside the Union whereas Dr. Joseph practised in Warrington. A resolution that the matter be referred to the House Committee was ruled out of order._

Mr. NV. C. BROOKS : May I ask how many members of the sib-committee decided upon this recommendation? The CHAIRMAN : There were two members of the committee.

Mr. ASHCROFT pressed for the names but the Chair- man did not see why they should be given. If Mr. Ashcroft had lost confidence in the committee he should say so point blank. Mr. HouGHTON said he was much surprised that the appointment had been made and he thought it would have been more courteous for the committee to have consulted the Board. He considered that the best course would be for. the resident medical officer to nominate his own deputy. The sub-committee had been going on too quickly and with some sense of discourtesy to the Board.

Mr. HICKM kti : I must raise my protest against the way in which special committees and other •committees are sometimes treated by this Board. Have you lost confidence in the committee ? If so tell us and we will disband without any resolution of yours. Mr. DAVIES : We have not lost confidence. _ _ • __

Mr. HICKM kN emphasised his protest. The sub-com- mittee, he added, spent hours in examining candidates for the resident medical officership and what considera- tion did the Board give to their recommendation? He would object to act on any committee if they were to be treated like that. The present decision was only a recommendation which rested with the Board to approve or otherwise.

In the course of further discussion it transpired that the committee instructed the clerk to send copies of the newspaper in which the appointment was advertised to certain local medical men. The reason, said the chairman, was that they expected the same difficulty that other Unions had experienced in getting men to do the work at the salary. The asail said there must be a deputy, and the Board had practically passed a resolution to nrovide one. If they did not wish to confirm this particular appointment they could refer it back, but it would be necessary to give notice before they could rescind the decision to appoint a deputy. Mr. JOLLEY said it was absolutely necessary, accord- ing to the consolidated orders, that there should be a deputy. Anyone with any regard for the welfare of the sick poor would surely feel strongly that the Board were inaugurating a great and important reform which would probably be not merely beneficial to the sick poor, but would perhaps reduce expenditure. Their first duty as Guardians was to see that that magnificent institution was properly administered. An honorary consult- ing staff would, he admitted, be an additional desirable reform, and there was mere than one medical man in Warrington who would be pleased to act in that capacity. A deputy was, however,very neces- sary and he had thought the committee's recommenda- tion would have been received with applause instead o f with carping criticism. He hoped they would not have any repetition of the recriminations against which Mr. Hickman had protested or any suggestion that anything had been done from improper :motives or with any other desire than the welfare of the inmates and the promotion of good administration. After further discussion, the appointment was con- firmed, only Mr. Ashcroft voting against it.

Suicide Through Grief.

A.n inquest was held by Mr. R. Dobson at the Walton Arms on Saturday on the body of John Smith (66),a retired gamekeeper, who committed suicide the previous day under pathetic circumstances. Edith Webster, a daughter of the deceased stated that she was in the kitchen at her father's house on Friday night about 9.30. Her father was sitting in his chair by the fire. He got up and turned to the fire, and witness's sister said he was cutting his throat. Witness got hold him from behind and tried to get the knife , from him, but could not. She then went for P.C. , Mellor, who lived next door, and he came in. The deceased's wife died on Thursday, which grieved him very much and caused him to be very strange in his manner.—P.C. Mellor said when he went to the house he found Smith bleeding. Witness laid him on his back and applied cold water bandages, and sent for the doctor who attended him. Smith lingered on for about two hours, when he died. The jury returned a verdict of "Suicide whilst of unsound mind."

SPELLING.

Lord ROSEBERY'S contribu- tion to the spelling reform controversy is not very important, and it indicates a preference rather than a conviction; but we are glad to welcome so able a speaker back to the platform on any terms. It is open to grave question whether any man of eminent public services, who has held two of the highest offices in the State, and who continues in full vigour alike of body and of intellect, is entitled to seclude himself as Lord ROSEBERY has done for a long time past. It would be difficult to redall a Case in which any man of equal rank in statesmanship has made so sad and complete a sacrifice of usefulness and of influence. But as the former Liberal Prime Minister declines any longer to take part in great affairs we welcome his occasional appearances in comparatively small ones. What he says is always fresh, generally amusing, and frequently suggestive. From his speech at Hawick the other night we learn, incidentally, that he is not in favour of the limitation of the speed of motor- cars. In that respect he is what he seldom becomes—of the same opinion as the crowd; for every owner of a motor-car, Mr. BALFOUR. particu- larly, is opposed to a limit on speed. We learn, too, or we may surmise, for he avoided any dis- tinct declaration on the subject, that he is in favour of reformed spelling. What sort of reform ? Here Lord ROSEBERY is as indefinite as he has some- times been in the expression of his political views. He recognises to the full all the difficulties of our present spelling; but we all do that. What divides us is want of harmony in opinion as to whether a change is necessary, or advisable, or possible On any generally-acceptable lines. Presi- dent ROOSEVELT'S plan of reform is not new. It is only more limited in extent than the plan on which it was founded, a plan which has never "caught on "in this country, though it has been propounded with great vigour and without ceasing for more than half-a-century. Systems of spelling reform repel the bulk of people because they ar3 unsightly. It is said, of course, that we should get used to them, as the folk of the sixties got used to crinolines. Almost everything loses its repulsiveness when it becomes familiar. Nothing looked more odd and ridiculous than a safety bicycle whilst the high bicycle was in fashion. Nowadays it is the high bicycle that would appear to be a monstrosity. But how many persons have become reconciled to a style of spelling in which the title of the late Sir ISAAC PITMAN'S "Phonetic News" seemed to be " Frantic Nuts ? there is always a strong objection to a change of spelling from the point of view of the student at language, for t of the spelling hitherto proposed would destroy the history of words. It was for this rc that Lord ROSEBERY desired to have the opinion of Dr- MURRAY, the editor of the great Dictionary, on the Roosevelt proposals ; But Dr. MranKY remained as silent on the subject of spelling as Lord ROSEBERY himself does on poli Here are two great men who refuse to give guid- ance. But the history of language difficulty could, ease than we think, be got over with much

objection to the mere look of such forms of as the phonetic Men and the abbreviators ae.The other day a humorist asked us to notice a certain famous line of BrJwning would look riuted according to the system adopted by SIDENT ROOSEVELT, and providentially ruled of court by the American Constitution : 0, link hw, haf angl and haf brtl, iat does that mean Could anybody, however dicacious, recognise the first lino of the beauti- ful apostrophe which the poet addresses to his wife: Oh, lyric love, half angel and half bird, And all a wonder and a wild desire! To the present generation of readers, at least, such a change in spelling would destroy the possibility of all delight in litersture. Lord ROSEBERY assumes that if we had a phonetic system we should all spell alike; but this by no means follows. Those persons who have a natural phonetic system, to whom his Lordship referred, have a system entirely their own. No two of them spell alike. Some day, per- haps, a phonetic system may be devised such as will command universal consent. Matters will then be made much easier for the child, though we doubt whether the difficulties of child- hood are not much exaggerated. The good spellers do not, as a rule, state by what means they learnt to spell. The faculty" growed," like Topsy in "Uncle Tom's Cabin.' Oa the other hand, the dad spellers would probably spell badly under any system. Lord ROSEBERY speaks of those who have now and then to write a word down several times before getting it right. That is really the case with all of us if we pause to think what is the proper form of a word. In matters of spelling he who hesitates is lost.

While an action is pending Tun PDT-Min between Colonel Edelsten PURCHASE. and the Corporation with regard to the compensation to be paid him for the removal of the pin-mill in Knutsford-road in order to allow of widening the thoroughfare, it is not advisable to discuss publicly the position of either party to the action. Counsels Opinion has been taken both by the Mayor and the Corporation, and upon those opinions the conduct of the Corporation will, presumably, be based. Our contemporary, the " Warrington Guardian," I created somewhat of a sensation on Saturday by (announcing that both counsel advised the Corpor- ation "to settle and carry out the agreement Which had been entered into as being the most ' economical course to adopt." Nothing could have been better calculated to prejudice the Corpora- tion's position than such a statement, and natur- allv many members of the Council were most Indignant. Moreover, it is denied that the state- Ment respecting counsels' opinion is correct. It ,was contended from the outset of the dispute that o.ls Corporation was not bound by the agreement, which had never been officially sealed, and we I believe that this contention can be upheld. That, however, is a matter for the Courts. In the iMeantime twenty members of the Council have .844 a requisition to the Mayor for a special meet- „lM: in order to consider the statement in the - Guardian "and pass resolutions thereon.

Success has not made LxnErtar, ACTIVITY. the Liberals in Warrington slothful—a fact which will Je frequently demonstrated during the coming ,Winter. Having won Warrington over to the cause of progress they mean to prevent it slipping ;lack into the rut of reaction. There is only one Way, and that is by work. Work won the election And work is to b 3 carried on unceasingly during the 1 'season at hand. "" Junior Liberal Association, as well as the parent, series body. on Monday the opening one of a of meetings was held under the auspices of I the first-named body and a large audience listened tO capital addresses from Mr. F. Neilson and Mr. J. Howes. Once again Mr. Neilson devoted him- self to a vigorous denunciation of the land-laws and argued for labour getting in closer touch with the land and sharing proportionately in the pro- ductiveness thereof. He could not hammer away at a better cause, and if Mr. Neilson goes on con- vincing audiences of the iniquity of the present ,Inonopoly in land he will bring appreciably nearer the taxation of land values and kindred reforms ~destined to improve the lot of the worker and ;reduce the burden of the ratepayer. A new era has dawned and we are hope ful—nay confident—that for many years to come the march of matters political will be rapid, and straight along the path which has the emancipa- tion of the democracy for its goal.

ceeded in obtaining at the hands of the sub- , committee was negatived by an odd vote. We are quite at a loss to know why the proposal— which provided that reports should only be required from the headmaster and managers in case of unsatisfactory work by assistants and that teachers implicated should have an oppor- tunity of defending themselves —should call forth such determined hostility. The teachers have justice on their side in objecting to systematic half- yearly reports. It appears to us a step savouring of the inquisatcnial that members of a worthy and hard-working profession should have to be reported upon individually in this way. There are no more conscientious servants of the public than the head-teachers of Warrington, and they can surely be relied upon to see that their assistants fulfil their duties satisfactorily. The head-teachers are held responsible for the efficiency of the schools nder their control, and for their own credit's sake they will be anxious for the best possible results to be obtained. We hope the local teachers will persist in their demands and that eventually the Education Committee will be prepared to look at things in a more reasonable light and thus avoid any possibility of friction with their servants.

islt an unfortunate circum- THE stance that the Warrington MUSICAL SOCIETY. Musical Society does not flourish. For a great many years now it has been one of Warrinv,ton's proudest institutions, and the debt owing to it by the public is incalculable. Doubtless the public is grateful for all that the Society and its self-sacrificing members d) in the way of providing high-class musical entertainment during the dreary winter months. But the sense of gratitude, however real it may be, is not being expressed in the most practical form and were it not for the immense popularity and success of the opera branch the Society would have difficulty in maintaining a bare existence. The opera provides the one bright feature in the financial aspect of the Society, for the revenue from the concerts is decreasing instead of increasing. Fortunately the Society lives to educate and entertain and not to make money. There is no fear of it surrendering the position it holds, and the musical public may still look forward to being regaled with high-class concert fare as long as the membership maintains its present excellent numerical standard.

What puzzles the linguist IDIOMATIC in speaking or writing a IDIOCY, foreign language is not grammatical knowledge so much as to acquire those modes of expression which are peculiar to every tongue, and cannot be translated literally into another. By persons unable to speak any tongue but their own, difficul- ties and charms of idornatic expressions are seldom appreciated. The apparent mistakes made by educated foreigners are laughed at without our realising that the Frenchman or the German has frequent occasion to feel amused by the perplexing and extraordinary ways in which Englishmen will use one word to imply all sorts of contrasting thoughts. Take for instance, the adjactive "clean." Now, that means something free from dirt or filth ; but when a doctor speaks of a " clean bill of health " the document which attests that fact may have upon it billions of harmful bacteria. When a criminal makes a "clean breast" of a murder he does not thereby purify himself. A horny-handed son of toil may have "clean hands" regarding some imputed evil, and yet they may be•literally black as a chimney-sweep's. A righteous person suffering from some cardiacal complaint may, nevertheless,be described as having a "clean heart." A blackguard who has picked one's ' pocket and is running off with all one's valuables may show a "clean pair of heels" although his boots are filthy. Then a person,whose language is always choice and never profane, may be said to have "a clean tongue," although, poor fellow, he may be suffering from cancer in that member. There is no language so rich in idiom as is English, and, therefore, none more puzzling to foreigners, who may understand and know our grammar as well as did Murray hirnself,and yet betray an alien nationality by a literal translation of his owu

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

It has been an easier business to reconcile England and France than it would be to make Manchester fraternise cordially with Liverpool, or Leeds with Bradford, or Edinburgh with Glasgow. As fierce as any is the feud between the Orkneys and Shetlands.— London Observer."

* * * Three miles of the London-Portsmouth road is to be divided into twelve quarter-mile portions, each of which is to be treated with different materials, to test them as dust-resisting road surfaces. It is claimed that the dustless road will be cheaper in the end than the present one. * * .

For an entainment at liishton ,on Saturday night the following preliminary notice was issued :—" In konexion with the forthcoming bazaar, a xibishun ov living statuary, grand mpire konsert, and f ruts bank- wet will be held on Saturday next, Sep. 15th, to komense at 7 o'klok prompt. A splendid Drogram has been arainjed. Admission 9d. ea3h. With apolojis to President Roosevelt and the nu speling."

Rumour is busy with the-names of possible candi- dates for the Mayoralty, but so far we have only heard of two likely gentlemen, viz. : Alderman Pierpoint and, Alderman Forataw. The former has been requested to accept office on more than one occasion in the past, and we are sure it would give general satisfaction if he could be prevailed upon this year to become Mayor of the Borough he has served so long and faithfully.

The German Opsies are still perambulating the country in an aimless sort of fashion. They have twice passed through Warrington this week, attended by the minions of the law in full force. Apparently they are so hurried by the police that they haven't time to wash. We should advise the attendance of the Corporation Fire Brigade with the hose, and Crosfields' Brigade with the soap, on the occasion of the next German invasion. They would do more real good than the police. * * * Another duty which may be added to the burden which fills the teacher's six hours a day is the superintendence of the scholars' teeth-cleaning. Mr. George Cadbury, with perhaps a feeling of compunc- tion at the havoc wrought by his toothsome chocolate, has offered to provide between five and six thousand tooth brushes and pots of tooth powder for the scholars in the King's Norton district. His proposition is that the cleansing operations shall be performed at the schools under the direction of the teachers. Eventu- ally they may have to wipe the scholars' noses—as we heard a teacher sarcastically remark not long ago when some other instance of a grandmotherly curri- culum was under discussion. * * Rarely have so many expressions of surprise been heard at a meeting of a Warrington public body as those which fell from the lips of members of the Board of Guardians on Wedndsday . In quick succes- sion members rose and gave vent to feelings of astonishment that a special committee, to whom they had delegated the arrangements for the appointment of resident medical officer, had seen fit to select a deputy as well. The committee stoutly protested that they were right in taking this wide conception of their powers and after a good deal of discussion, the Board came to the conclusion that a deputy was quite necessary and sanctioned the new appointment,though many considered that the committee had acted rather hastily.

* * * The Warrington Branch of the National service League brought off its annual meeting at Appleton on Saturday with considerable eclat. There were lords and ladies in uncommon plenty, but a rather noticeable scarcity of the humble individual who will carry the gun if the League's proposals ever come to pass. However, this may be explained by the fact that the meetinc, was not a public one. There was plenty of speech-making, which it would perhaps be unkind to designate as a beating of the air. Still, we cannot help thinking that, though the League is increasing and doing most energetic propagandist work, it will never attain its object. The tendenry of the times appears to be more and more in tlsio direction of disarmament and not national gum. carrying.

The Examiner, Saturday, September 22, 1906 a ST HELENS.

' Dr. J. W. Townsend,an ex-president of the National Free Church Council, in referring to the evil of betting at Blackburn on Tuesday night,said he wished our noble King could only be influenced by the Christian sentiment of the country to give up his racing pro- pensities, and withhold his countenance, his presence and his patronage from that which was an overwhelm- ing curse to large numbirs of the nation. If he did so, he was sure he would be by far the greatest King we had had since the days of William 111. * *

An amusing incident is related in connection with the Oddfellows' bowling main. So generous were the donors of the prizes that all competitors who sur- vived the third round received a reward. In the first round one trundler was given a bye and the same good fortune attended him in the second stage. In the third round his opponent failed to pat in an appear- ance so that he became entitled to a prize —a bottle of whisky—without having delivered a single wood; It was felt, however, that he had not earned his reward and at the prize distribution on Thursday the bottle was opened and its contents disposed of by those present at the gathering. The only regret felt was that a box .of cigars did not go with the whisky.

0 4i, The delegates of the Free Churches attached to the South-West Lancashire Federation at Thursday's con- ference showed that they were fully alive to the questions of the day and gave the impression that most of them have studied to advantage many of the religious and social problems which are in the forefront. They are very business-like in their methods, and get through their work in a rapid manner —this arising we believe from two reasons, viz : that the delegates are generally chosen by the Churches for their business abilities and also that they make a point of endeavouring to arrive at a common agreement, and if they cannot then being agreeable to each other over their differ ences. We commend this latter grace to some of our other local governing bodies. * * * _ _

We are accustomed in th-ese days to speak of assets in matters other than commercial undertakings. For example, we sometimes say that one of the Most valuable assets to the nation, is our children. We also say—at least the Imperialists do—that the British flag is one of our finest commercial assets, and we, also say that the finest assets of individual life are health and moral strength. Applying the question of assets in another way, we should say that in Mr. Charles E. Parker, of Penketh, the Free Churchmen have a very valuable asset. He has taken a very keen and practical interest in the work now for some years, and in both the Free Church's religious and social side he has been locally one of its most prominent figures. On Thursday at the meeting of the Siuth- West Lancashire Federation of Free Churches (fully reported in another column) he proved an admirable chairman, and not only so, but he proved to be a most ' hospitable host, for he entertained the delegates of the various Councils to luncheon at the Winmarleigh Cafe, and he was thoughtful enough not to omit the members of the Fourth Estate. Mr. Parker in many ways demonstrates his public spirit, and we can only wish that more citizens would follow his example.

Football and Bowling. I.L P. Debate. . The Independent Labour Party commenced its debating class on Wednesday evening. A paper was read by Mr. Edmund Osborne on the subject of "Trade Unionism and Socialism." The subsequent discussion was taken part in by Council lor Stott, Messrs. Horrocks, Pierce Williams, Lowe and others. Mr. Osborne was complimented on the excellence of his paper and a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to him at the close of the meeting. Music in the Park. The Borough Brass Band will play the following programme of music in the Park on Sunday : —Hymn, Rothwell," G. Wadsworth; march, "Honest Toil," W. Rimmer ; selection, Rossini, H. George Platt, a married man, residing on the Manchester-road at Woolston, and employed as an engineer at the Corporation Electric Lighting Station, Rowley, was attending one of the generators on lion- day evening when he received a shock of 6,000 volts. P.C.'s Turner and Newey conveyed him to the Infir- mary in the horse ambulance and he was detained as an in-patient. J, R. WOOD, Piano Dealer and Tuner, 47, Old London-road., Stockton Heath.—Advt. Death of Mrs. G. W. Clubley. Many of our readers will regret to hear of the death of Mrs. Clubley, wife of Mr. G. W. Clubley, manager at Messrs. Marshall and Wright's, Bridge- street, who passed away last Friday after several weeks' illness. The interment took place at the Warrington Cemetery on Sunday, the Rev. W. Brace- camp officiating and delivering a feeling address. STILL LEADING, the "HERALD" KITCHEN RANGES and " PEVERIL " FIRE GRATNS Sole Agent.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge-st., Warrington.—Advt. Death of a Local Teacher. Many residents in the Bank Quay district will regret to hear of the death of Mrs. Fen iday, Thewlis-street, who passed away early this (Friday) morning. The deceased was for many years a teacher at St. Barnabas' day school and latterly occupied a similar position at Lowton. For some time she had been in failing health but it was only during the past few days that she had been confined to the house. She was much esteemed by a large circle and her death will be greatly regretted. Billiard Champions at Warrington. During the week a billiard match of 8,000 up has been in progress at the Lion Hotel, the contestants being Tom Toothill, boy champion of England, and Dick Chambers, boy champion of Ireland. The play has been followed with great interest and last (Thurs- day) evening the score was : —Toothill, 5,223 ; Chambers, 4,804. On Thursday Toothill made breaks of 113 (his highest for the week), 93 and 85. Chambers' best break of the match is 53. Labour Candidate for Orford. Hampson and Mr. Pook, the Socialist and Labour candidate.

Labourer Badly Injured, Peter Higgins, aged 40, residing at 22, Ship-yard, and employed by the Bishop's Wharf Shipping Co., sustained serious injuries whilst following his employment on Monday. He was worling at the wharf when two bags of barley which were being loaded fell upon him. He was conveyed in the horse ambulance to the Infirmary by P.C.Muskett,where it was found that, both thigh bones were broken and that he had a contused wound on the chest. He was detained in the institution.

S. Hughes presided. RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

"FREE SPEECH "AT WIDNES. Before the Widnes magistrates, on Monday Mr. Daniel Garghan, the recently defeated Labour candidate for Waterloo Ward, applied for the second time to the bench for the enforcement of the penalty against him for obstruction in the recent "free speech " crusade.— The Mayor : We cannot help ourselves.—Mr. Garghan asked that they should enforce the penalty which had been put upon him. He was willing to go to gaol, as he didn't intend to pay. It had been hanging over him for seven or eight weeks, and the police were harassing him and causing crowds of people to assemble at his house.—The Clerk: No doubt you will go in time. Sit down.—Mr. Garghan: It is net justice.

WIDNES COUNCILLOR LOSES HIS VOTE.

At the Town Hall, Widnes, Mr. J. T. Foard, revising barrister, held his revision of voters' lists on Monday evening.—Councillor Charles Hemingway applied for a succession vote to 85, Church-street, from 39.—The claim was objected to by Mr. John Williamson, Liberal agent.—Mr. Williamson: When did you leave 39 Church-street ?—Councillor Hemingway : In October last—Mr. Williamson : Where did you go to after that? —I went into apartments.—How long were you in apartments ?—Councillor Hemingway made no answer, but produced a rent book.—Betweeu October and May you were a lodger ?—Yes; I had to sell my house and get it ready for sale.— Then, unfortunately, you lose your vote.—Why ?—The Revising Barrister : Because you were in apartments and apartments were not mentioned in the list. You are living at 85, Church-street, and you went there in May of this year. If you move during the qualifying year you have to state that. You did not come straight from Church-street. You have not been long enough at 85 to get a vote. You will get it next year, not this. The fact that you have been in apartments shows there has been a break in the occupation, and that is fatal.— Councillar Hemingway: I was on the list last year.— The Revising Barrister : It is unfortunately the point that you have ceased to be a householder.

REDUCTION IN THE RATES.

The Clerk submitted the estimates of expenditure for the coming half year,which was; general expenses £3,905, contingencies and amount to meet expenses of subject half year before a further call be made £3,550 ; total £7,455. Deducting the probable balance of the present half year there was left to be provided out of the rat.s £4275, equal to a rate of 6d. in the 2, a reduction of 2d. Mr. Woodward moved the adoption of the estimates. _ - .

IMr. Linaker seconded. The ieduction in the rates was due to the good administration of the Council and they had nothing to thank the County Council for. He did not think the County Council call would be any lege ' that it had been. I The motion was carried. SHOCKING FATALITY AT WIDNE.S. A sensation was created at Widnes on Saturday after- noon by the news that a fatal accident had occurred to a young married man named James Voes, a brakesman on the London and North-Western Railway, and who resided at 45, Greenway-road, Widnes. At half-past one he left home in good health and spirits to go to his shunting operations at the old Marsh Siding, Widnes. , At three o'clock he was doing some shunting at what is I known as the New Road. James Stott, a brakesman, of I 8, Cooper-street, was engaged in a similar operation, shunting nine waggons on another line from a Crewe train to a new line. At that time Voes was sitting at the side of the line. Stott saw three waggons shortly afterwards pass over the spot where Voes had been sitting. Turning round to reverse the points, he saw Voes was missing. The waggons were bumping, and he thought that something was wrong, and, prooeeding there, he saw the man's mutilated body on the rails. Calling out for assistance, Walter Potmore, another brakesman, of Charles-street came upon the scene. The body was then removed to the mortuary of the Accident Hospital, and it was found that the poor fellow's injuries were of a shocking character, almost the whole of his body being smashed. He was a respectable,steady young man,and had been in the employ of the company for eight years. The case is all the more distressing from the fact that he leaves a young widow and a child , about three weeks old.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON

GROCERS' EARLY CLOSING. An adjourned meeting of grocers was held in the Town Hall, Earlestown, last week, for the further dis- cussion of early closing of shops on Saturday night. Although 8 o'clock was in the majority, it was finally decided to close at 9 o clock and to commenee the first Saturday in October. It was earnestly requested that the Dublic would consider the lengthy hours that shop- keepers and their assistants have to work espeeially at the week ends, and that they would do their shopping earlier, enabling trodespeople of all kinds to close their shops and by so doing shorten the hours of labour for both men and masters.

MR. SEDDON M.P. AND HIS VOTE. SEQUEL TO A WIDNES SPEECH.

At the St. Helens Revision Court last Thursday, the vote of Mr. James Andrew Seddon, M.P. for the Newton Division, was brought prominently before the Revising Barrister, Mr. J. T. Foard. The Conservatives had objected to Mr. Seddon's vote in respect of his house in Lingholme-road.—Mr. Jeffrey added that in the course of a;speech Mr. Seddon said:—" Personally, I have no property to seize ; my wife, who is a better housekeeper than I am, owns the lot. I have nothing. They can only take this (pointing to himself amidst cheers.)" He (Mr. Jeffrey), submitted that on Mr. Seddon 's own showing, and from his own words it wrs sufficient to make an objection against him. He would not have taken that objection had not Mr. Seddon used those words himself. Since the objection was made, however, he had, with Mr. Wilson visited the landlord's representative and seen the rent books, and he found that what Mr. Seddon had said was not exactly correct.—The Barrister : That is, what I assumed at ones. People sometimes say a goofi deal in the excitement of a speech that they could lilt vouch for in an affidavit.—Mr. Jeffreys said that theignestion also arose as to whether Mr. Seddon could claim a vote, because it was still a matter of argument whether a man could be the tenant of his owl 2 wife. So long as the wife held the whole of the furniture the landlord could not go ant take his furniture, because he had none, and therefore could not distrain. The Barrister : I think it is very doubtful. In the first place, you have not given Mr. James Andrew Seddon notice to appear here.—Mr. Jeffrey said that in that spirit of conciliation which had been taken up on the part of his committee this year. they had agreed with the landlord to look upon Mr. Seddou as the tenant, and they had decided to withdraw the objection but at the same time he (Mr. Jeffrey) had had quite sufficient grounds for making his objection.—The Barrister.: We are quite at one. You have stated your case, and I admit at once it was quite right. "A mow BELOW THE BELT."

Mr. Seddon, M.P.:, was one of the speakers at a politi- cal meeting at St: Helens on Thursday night. He opened his speech by addressing his hearers as fellow- electors." I can call you that," he said, "because the magnanimous Tory Party to-day have condescended to allow me to be a free-born Britisher, and to have the privileges of one." Owing to his action for free speech in Widnes, he continued, the Tory Party thought locally that he was not the tenant of a house, and saw the opportunity to strike a blow below the belt. However, when they made inquiries, they found that their objec- tion was not good. He always found that when the Tory Party assumed the role of Uriah Heap, and met obstacles which they could not remove, they were generous to a fault. Their generosity was overpower- ing, and so far as the Tory agent was concerned he (Mr. Seddon) publicly begged to thank him for that which he could not help. He had had a free show that da.3, because he (Mr. Seddon) he did not happen to be there. Had he been there the wigs would have been on the green. He could therefore, call them "fellow-citizens."

------- - The Late Mr. Henry Taylor's Successor.

After much consideration the Police Court and Prison Gate Mission of the Diocesan C.E.T.S., have' appoinied as the successor to the late Mr. Henry Taylor as Police Court Missionary for Warrington, Borough and County, Mr. John James Firth, who is now attending in a similar capacity the Court of Mr. W. J. Stewart, the Liverpool Stipendiary Magistrate. Mr. Firth has had considerable experience in Police Court and Prison Gate Mission work. Warrington is to be congratulated on his selection. Mr. Firth, who will reside at Latchford, will commence his work at the Borough Court on Monday next.

• Trap Accident. Mr. Joseph Palmer, a director of the English Sewing Cotton Company, who lives out at Lymm, met with a serious accident whilst driving on Saturday evening with his friend Mr. Faulkner. They were almost back home when someone shook a tablecloth outside a cottage door, at which the horse suddenly shied, running the trap up a bank and pitching Mr. Faulkner and Mr. Palmer into the road. Unluckily, Mr. Palmer got a leg through the spokes of the trap, and when he was taken home it was found that he had sustained a compound fracture. Mr. Faulkner was little the worse for the mishap.

UNDER THE CLOCK for Good BOOTS. Advt.— Warrington and District Shorthand Writers' Association.

The usual weekly meeting was held at the Winmarleigh Cafe on Wednesday night, there being an excellent attendance, particularly of the gentler sex, with whom shoithand appears to be a very popular subject Speed practice was conducted, matter being I read at the respective rates of GO, 80 and 100 words per minute. Considering that this was only the Association's second meeting, the amount of earnest- ness exhibited in trying to keep pace with the reader was very praiseworthy. There is no doubt that shortly several of the members who attend the short- hand classes at the Technical Institute will be showing their fellow-students " how to do it." A paper was read by the Hon. Secretary (Mr. Joseph Marsh) on an important phonographic subject, techni- cally known as "The Halving Principle," and proved very entertaining, this being one of a series which will be given in explanation of various knotty points in the shorthand text books. Mr. Llewellyn Davies gave a lecture entitled "Literature," his chief contention being that too much time was given nowadays to the reading of ordinary everyday novels which are turned out by the score while n)t enough time spent with the standard authors, Carlyle, Scent, Dickens, and the works of the " Immortal Bard." lip also "had a go" at the very cheap and trasy novelettes and magazines that are now so much in evidence, and in the discussion which followed the general opinion seemed to be that it would certainly be more beneficial to read the works of the standard authors, but that considerable benefit and no little instruction could be obtained from the perusal ef good novels and several of the monthly magazines. To those who are in doubt as to the advantages to be derived from a knowledge of short- hand, the lecture to be given next week entitled "The educational influences of phonography " should be of considerable asssistance.

===== 0002601_19060922_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

[By " Ovalist."] Warrington's unbeaten record still stands, and it is some credit to them to have vice gone away from home and emerged with points from the-ordeal. On Saturday they were at St. Helens and gained the victory they anticipated after a hard straggle. The Saints were no match for Warrington in point of cleverness, but they played with great determination and kept Warrington fully occupied. The final score of 19 points to 10 points just about indicates the difference in class between the two teams.

Play was of a very lively description from start to finish and the spectators got plenty of excitement for their money. The first half was undoubtedly the best from a football point of viecs. Both sides played an open game and Warrington got plenty of opportunities for showing their speed and combination. As a result they put on a goal and three tries in very quick time, and all the Saints could credit themselves with were a goal and a try due to Briers charging down a kick under the 'Warrington posts. In the second half, Warrington had to resume without Berry and for about a quarter of an hour they were eompletely over- played. St. Helens had a slight advantage forward from the commencement and when Thomas went into the back division they dominated the play entirely. Warrington were exceedingly hard preskd,and a little steadiness by the home backs must have brought two or three tries as its reward. The visitors escaped luckily more than once, but eventually a big rash resulted in another try under the posts, which was converted, and St. Helens were only a point behind.

At this stage the home spectators shouted themselves I hoarse, and Warrington looked like being pushed over the rails. There was a yell of delight when Prescott I marked nearly in front of goal, which changed into hooting of a pronounced character when the referee ' , ordered a scrummage. Personally I thought the mark ' was a fair one, but the man was bowled over so quickly that the referee may not have seen the catch properly. Anyhow the incident ended the home team's chance of getting in front. Berry resumed his position again and Warrington quickly git the upper hand. Within the next ten minutes another goal and two tries were put on by Fish and Isherwood and the issue made safe.

Judging from this game, in which there was a lot of keenness due to local rivalry, the " play the ball " rule will cause trouble unless the referees are unani- mous in their rulings and prompt in their decisions. More than once, when two or three men came down together the man with the ball got little chance of playing it, and some of the forwards did 1110 t seem particular whether they played the man or

r not. In a tangle like this the whistle should sciata I instantly for a scrummage. In another case, a St. Helens man was grassed with the ball near his own. line. There was not a " Saint" within yards so he I hung on to the oval like grim death till a few comrades I arrived on the scene. Really he should have been penalised, though one could sympathise with him in his plight. Playing the ball meant giving a try away, which is just what Naylor did once when strictly eon— forming to the rules. I see that Mr. J. H. Smith awarded a try on Saturday to Halifax because a Broughton man threw the ball into touch instead of " playing "it in the proper way. The St. Helens "A" team full back did the same thing at Warrington on. Saturday when he was in a tight corner. It looked a good way out of the difficulty, but it is not football. Several lessons of this kind require to be well learnt by both referees and players, before the game is played as it should be.

The reduction in the number of scull:lmages under ; the present was rules most marked in Saturday's game. ; In fact a few more would have been an improvement, for there was far too much pitnting and following up. St. Helens attempted little else,but kick and rush in the second half, and as several showers made the hall I wet the tactics payed. The Rugby game is dependent largely on the scrummage for its distinctive character and it would be a pity if the scrummage were almost legislated out of Northern Union football. Under better conditions and with two clever teams operating play would probably bo more on orthodox line. On Saturday it was neither one thing nor the other for half the game, and a casual observer might have thought the rival codes of Rugby and Association had got mixed in some mysterious fashion.

Dealing with individuals, praise could be given honestly to all the Warrington backs. Fish roused himself and gave the sort of exhibition which one expects from the captain of the team and a player of his reputation. He looked for work on Saturday, found plenty, and got three tries as a due reward. His second, following on fine play by Hockenhall and Isherwood, was such as only he can score. Three men lay in wait as he came down touch, but Jack waltzed round them in the old sweet way amid deafening cheers. Now he has got going he must keep up the pace. Under the present rules he should get more tries than ever he did. After Fish, Isher- wood was the best man on the field whether in attack or defence and has seldom done better work . The forwards were a bit overplayed in the second half but stuck well to their work. On the home side, Hillen at centre did a double share,while Toole was clever at half back. Atkinson, notwithstanding his bald pate and general air of hoary antiquity, played a sound full back game and the Saints need look no further while he is fit and well.

There is just one point where I think Warrington were lacking in tactics. Much of the kicking was too straight. A little cross-punting, with wings like Fish and Brooks, would have paid handsomely, for the chances are all on the side of the man following the ball. Even if it rolls in touch something is gained. Albert Goldthorpe was an adept at this art under the old rules, and judging by the way he led his side to victory at Bradford on Saturday, he has found it still to pay. As a matter of fact there is more demand and more scope for head-work of this kind than ever there was. I hope Warrington will not be slow to learn.

The home clubs were seen to much advantage on Saturday, for in only two instances were visiting thirteens (Warrington and Hunslet) allowed to take back points. The aggregate scoring was again of big dimensions, for in the 13 games played 400 points were scored, 304 being to the credit of the winning and 90 to the defeated teams. The average number of points in every game played so far this season works out at nearly 30, and spectators, at any rate, cannot complain of lack of incident in a scoring sense. In the 42 games played on the first three Saturdays of last season 569 points were scored. In the 39 games played this season 1,219 points have been scored. Figures likkhese speak for themselves.

Another easy victory for Warrington should be recorded this afternoon when Widnes visit the Wilders- pool enclosure. Luckily the home team is still unchanged, though it was feared that Berry's injury at St. Helens might force him to stand down. The thirteen who have done duty in each match so far, and who are again selected are as follows : Berry, full back ; Fish, Isherwood, Taylor and Brooks, three- , (wafters ; Hockenhall and Let, half-hacks ; Preston, Boardman, Thomas, Heath, \ Hara:Pr and Naylor forwards.

The" A " team will have an off-day and endeavour to learn something by watching the seniors whack Widnes.

E The Warrington men most likely to be chosen are apparently Naylor and llockenhall. This season has found Naylor in the best form of his life, and the ex-guardsman has my best wishes for County honours. Such men as Boardman, Thomas, Ferguson (Oldham), and Poole (Broughton) are not mentioned, while even Fish is only on the side of the Possibles. Perhaps a few experiments are being tried for there are quite a number of new names. D. Isherwood and S. Lees are among the reserves.

Cycling. The Warrington Cycling Club brought to a close one of the most successful seasons on record on Saturday, when thirty members assembled at Sankey Chapel House. A bowling main was played, the prize-winners being :-1, J. Podmore ; 2, Taylor; 3, G. Watkins; 4, J. Valentine. Tea was served, and afterwards a most enjoyable smoking concert was held. Several little speeches of congratulation on the success of the season were delivered.

LOCAL CHURCHES.

Continued from page 6. St. Paul's Church.

The foundation stone of the new vestry at St. Paul's Church will be laid this (Saturday) afternoon with clue ceremony by Mrs. Smethurst. The proceedings will commence at 3.30.

VINERY RAIDED. John Hewitt, Jame Moore, and James Henshall were remanded at St. Helens on Monday on charges of steal- ing and receiving grapes, fowl, ..Frc. The Chief Constable said that on September Bth there were twenty-eight [ bunches of grapes stolen from the vinery of Mr. James Lewis, Brooklands, Eccleston. Sergeant Holding made inquiries and found that Hewitt had been seen with grapes and tomatoes, and his brother made a statement that they had been stolen from Mr. Lewis's. Moose was arrested after Hewitt had been secured, and it was then found that they had been stealing hens at a wholesale rate, and that Hensball had been dealing with them. Mr. Wilson, fruiterer, of Denton's Green, said that he had had twenty-six fowls stolen, and Richard Whitfield, of Church-road, Eccleston, said he had some hens stolen on Wednesday night, August 2nd. When arrested the men made statements incriminating themselves. The , men were remanded for eight days.

St. James' Church.

The re-opening services in connection with St. James' Church were continued on Sunday, when large congre- gations assembled. At the evening service the musical portion was of a specially attractive character, and included the anthem " Magnify His Name." The bass solo was taken by Mr. Hill, and the duet by Messrs. Hill and Pevett. Mr. E. Davies presided at the organ. The Vicar conducted the service and the lessons were read by Mr. StansP.eld. The preacher was the Arch- deacon of Macclesfield (Archdeacon Wood), who preached from the words :—" Therefore let no man"glory in men. For all things are yours ; whether Paul, or Apollos, or Cephas, or the world, or life or death, or things to come all are yours, and ye are Christ's and Christ is God's."—T. Cor. iii.2l, 22, and 23.

iNsclitte P.S.A. Society. There vas a fair attendance at the meeting on Sunday when Mr. R. Lewis presided, and spoke upon gratitude to God for all His Blessings. There were, he said, none of them but had great cause, for thankfulness for all God's mercies and blessings. Mr. Ashworth of Hey- wood addressed the meeting on " Lessons from King Uzziah an his visions." The temple he said had its places of approach, for the Gentiles, the women, and the great Tsrrel—and finally the Holy place. So in our places of life, there was a place for us to unite in our Worship to God, the business of life, the politics of our day, with a devotion and worship. Consecrating all our talents to God, we should see like King I.lzziah, visions of God. Our best, physically, morally. and spiritually, would be acceptable to God. Mr. W. R. Beswick and Mr. A. Healey also took part in the met- ing. Miss Jessie Farrar presided at the piano. 19s. 6d. was received for the organ fund. The Secretary,heartily welcomed three new members, the total now being over 470. He uraed the brethren to do their best to help to crowd the Wycliffe Church on Sunday afternoon, when a special collection will be taken for the Organ Fund.

LECTURE ON CANADA.

An interesting lecture on Canada was delivered by Mr. A. Ward. a justice of the peace for Toronto, and who has resided in Canada nearly 20 years, on Monday night at the Earlestown Town Ilan. The Rev. T. R. Matthews presided, and there was a large attendance. The lecturer, whose remarks were illustrated by lime- light views, produced from photographs taken by him- self, spoke of Canada's climate, natural beauty, and its wonderful prospects. He referred to its remarkable increase in population, and to the loyalty of the Canadians to ther mother country, observing that no one in England recognised the greatness of the Empire's power whilst they remained in England. At the con- •lusion of the lecture several cinematograph pictures were show.

===== 0002601_19060922_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
ACCIDENT AT VIADUCT WORKS.

James Parrott, of Sankey-street, Earlestown, met with a serious accident on Saturday, whilst following his employment at the minder of a stationary engine at the Viaduct Works. He was inspecting a lift which was working rather stiffly when it suddenly descended and terribly crushed his right foot. He was conteyed borne and attended by Dr. Watkins, and it was found that the amputation of four toes was necessary.

WOMAN'S REALM.

DRP.SS-FASHION-HOME.

PETTICOAT CORSETS.

Stays and petticoats in one appeal strongly to the Parisians, and have much patronage in Eng- land. For wearing with Princess dresses they are simply invaluable, says the Queen, the foundation of the figure is everything. We owe a deep debt of gratitude to the clever dressmaker who evolved the petticoat corset from her brain. These one-piece garments, difficult as they are to make, have many merits. This one has origina- ted in the necessity of dispensing with every item of superfluous underclothing. The under-gar- ments mean a thin silk combination, and over this the corset petticoat, some having filmy chemise and drawers between. The new garment is most elaborately trimmed, it fits the figure to the knee, and then expands to flouncings and frillings of all kinds. The corsets fasten at the back, a yoke-piece is cut to the stays below the waist, and the petticoat attached is often made in rich brocade. SMART AUTUMN COSTUME.

Pictured in our illustration is a particularly smart and becoming little costume intended for autumn and early winter wear. The material of which it is fashioned is a pretty tweed in the fashionable colour combination of brown and grey. The coat is made in bodice fashion, and is taken into a jaunty little belt of soft brown leather. Round the neck runs a strapping of the material edged with a row of stitching, and finished with a brown leather button in each point. The front of the coat consists of a wide box-pleat, flanked on either side with two wide tucks, all machine-stitched at the edges, the box-pleat being further adorned with bcown

leatber buttons. The coat is set inte a smart pointed yoke on the shoulders, each point being ornamented with a leather button. The sleeve, of elbow length, are finished with deep gauntlet cuffs. The skirt, close-fitting over the hips, is perfectly plain save for a panel front stitched and flanked with tucks to match the coat. ELDERLY WIVES AND YOrNG HrSBANDS.

Now and then, says P.T.0., a tender-hearted woman will wed with her junior—perfectly aware she is staking her all—just because she is desperately in love. "I bring him money, un- derstanding, sympathy, and absolute devotion," she tells herself; "he is in love with me now." It is the "now" that is so pitiful, for now is not always, and though twenty-one is often dazzled by thirty-three, thirty-one, as a rule, admires girls in the twenties. So the elderly wife in love tries to cheat time, and frisks with the lambs. Her faded face looks out from under girlish mil- linery, and she wears herself out by trying to i be tho young, playful companion. It s a truism that people who may safely except themselves from a general rule are the exceptional people. To certain singularly exquisite and refined affec- tions disparity of age has less to say than to the rank and file. Peculiar temperaments, too, have peculiar affinities. A sedate, studious young man, who values peace and comfefrt before ro- mance, will often deliberately select a woman considerably his elder, and live perfectly content. .

A MODEL SHIRT BLOUSE.

Smart aisd workmanlike shirt blouses promise to be quite the most modish thing for morning wear during the coming autumn and winter. .1 The pretty model pictured in our sketch is quite of the latest and most becoming type, and is easily and quickly made. The material of which it is fashioned is fine ivory cashmere, very light in weight. Down the front of the blouse rune a wide box-pleat edged with a finger-width strapping of brown washing silk. no box-pleat Pierced with slots at intervals

to allow of the pulling through of a pretty brown silk stock. A deep, shaped yoke, coming over the edges of the box-pleat, forms the upper part of the blouse, its edges being strapped with the brown washing silk. On to this yoke the blouse proper is gathered. The sleeves, of the bishop type, are finished at the wrists with deep pointed cuffs edged with a strapping of the brown silk.

SHYNESS.

_ It is not always easy for a young and inexperi- enced wife, who is not quite sure of what is the correct thing to do and say, to fulfil the many duties that are required of a hostess, both in the matter of receiving visitors and in the entertain. ment of guests. Especially is this the case should she be at all nervous or suffer from shy- ness. It is very strange the way that shyness affects different natures, says "Lady Clare" in the Lady. Some people under its influence seem to forget everything they have to do or say; others,. again, become cold and stiff in manner, while in contrast to these there are some who simply from shyness are so brusque and curt in speech that they often astonish those around them by saying and doing ;the most unconven- tional things; and yet all these different effects are caused by one and the same uncomfortable feeling.

TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

GROUSE PlE.—Pick, draw, and singe two or three young grouse, cut off the wings and put the drumsticks through a slit under the thigh, divide each bird in four pieces, fry them till half cooked in butter, and season them with cayenne, mace, salt, and pepper. While frying these in another saute pan fry some collops of tender rump steak, seasoning the with chopped mushrooms, shal- lots, pepper, and salt; arrange them in the pie. dish, place the grouse neatly upon it, also some hard-boiled eggs cut in quarters; fill the dish with good gravy, cover with puff paste. sad bake

PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

THE ROYAL ROAD TO LEARNING.

Before Queen Victoria ascended the throne she had to devote a great deal of time to her studies. These she pursued with a fair amount of diligence, though she would sometimes attempt to display her independence by refusing to act according to the rules that had been laid down for her benefil and guidance. On one occasion, whilst learning the pianoforte, she objected to an exercise that had been put before her. She was told that although the lesson might seem dull, yet it was necessary that she should go through it before she could become mis- tress of the piano. "Then what would you think of me," she asked, "if I became mistress at once?"

The Princess was told that that was impossible: there was no royal road to music. "Oh, there is no royal road to music, eh 7" repeated the princess. No royal road! And I am not mistress of my pianoforte? But I will be I assure you! And the royal road is this." Whereupon she: closed the piano, locked it, and took out the key. " There!" she continued with a smile, "that's be- ing mistress of the piano. And the royal road to learning is never to take a lesson till you're in the humour to do it." A few moments afterwards, to show that she spoke more in fun than in opposi- tion to the wishes of her tutor, she unlocked the piano, and sitting down, resumed the interrupted kaaoth

WHY " POLLY ?`

'Why are parrots called " Polly?" One writer, who objects to the habit as a "silly practice," says that this name arose several hundred years ago. Some sailor on his return to England from a port in tropical Africa brought with him a strange talking bird, which he had named "Polly," after his sweetheart. The perfermances of the bird exciting the admiration of those who heard them, the name, it is said, became popular as a familia, title for parrots generally.

A "QtTANKY " TRIO. Never was seen such a wonderful threte As stood on the shore of the Sandlewpocl Sea, While sounds that would make a deaf man star* Rose and fell on the crinklesome air. 'Twas the Gryvelling Gryg of the fan-like ear, And the spidery legs so flink and grear; 'Twits the Winnying Wix with his smile so bland, And the Spotted Guffoon from the Tumtum Land. They sat on the shore of the Sandlewood Sea, The quankiest trio that e'er could be; And they yowled and yowled with a keen delight From eight in the morning till eight at night. Why did they do it? Ah, who's to say? Oddities act in their own quaint way. And if you inquire if they still are there, Well, I neither know and I neither care.

STORY OF KING ALFONSO.

Had he only been a boy and not King of Spain, the remarks of Alfonso XIII. when quite little would still have been entitled to a place among the quaint sayings of children. His Majesty had chicken at dinner one day, and finding it hard work to manage it properly with his fork and spoon, he quietly laid these down pieked up a bit of the fowl with his fingers, and began to eat. . . .

, Thereupon the attendaut rebuked him, saying, "Kings do not eat with their fingers." To which Alfonso made reply, perhaps with a twinkle in his eye, "This King does, and he went on with his sinner.

SOLOMON'S BIRDS.

Hoopoes so named from their cry are a well- known bird in Central and Southern Europe, in Africa, and other parts of the world. The most noticeable thing About their appearance is their fine crest of feathers on their heads. Charles Kingsley shy:, they were called Solomon's birds, and for the following reason : At one time, so the story goes,. they all. wore golden crowns, with the result that men hunted I n d slew them for these same crowns of gold, until life itself bocame a burden to the poor birds. Out there came a day when the birds and the beasts went to the Court of Solomon to pay homage to the wise king; and the hoopoes resolved to bring their hardships before the monarch's notice.

They were allowed to do so, and accordingly they begged that the Ring would pray that they might weer any kind of crown but their golden ones. Solomon took pity on them and prayed, and the hoopoes received crowns of feathers instead of gold. And now they lead a cheerful, merry life, and have also the honour of being called after the far-famed king.

===== 0002601_19060922_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ELEPHANT AND THE BABY.

The elephant is often employed in India tt watch baby, and very proud the big animal is of the confidence reposed in it.. When the child cries the elephant is much more distressed than a nurse- maid would be, and should its tiny friend take to thumping it more in anger than in sorrow, the tusker pays no heed to the rain of puny blows. In one instance an elephant grew so fond of its charge that it would take its food only in the pre- sence of the bairn. Parents do not fear to leave their children in the keeping of elephants of proved fidelity and good temper.

SLUMBER SONG.

To slumberland our craft we steer, Baby and I together. Slowly, but surely, our port we near. Baby and I together. When the dream tree spreads its branches wide, And scatters its fruit on either side, Down the twilight river we float along, While lapping waves croon a tender song Baby and I together. A fair little head is drooping low, Baby and I together. Gently into the harbour go Baby and I together. Have reached the shores of Slumberland, By whispering breezes softly fanned, Amid the fleet that are anchored fast, Hush! we are safely moored at last, Baby and I together. WHAT A WEE LAD BECAME.

Little Archie Tait was rather a delicate boy Lila so was not sent to school as soon as he would have been had been strong and well. , But he learned lessons at home, and sometimes when he could get no one else to hear him say his Latin grammer, he used to ask his old nurse, Betty Morton, to assist him. Although she did not understand a word of it, she would hold the book up to her eyes, and now and then remark, "Ay, ay, it maim be richt, for it's just sword for word.' Or if he made a mistake, she corrected him, saying, "Na, na ; it's no' that at all." And yet this was Scottish lad in course of time became headmaster of Rugby, then Bishop of London, and died Arch- bishop of Canterbury.

\ WARRINGTON

Labouree Badly Injured,

Peter Higgins, aged 40, residing at 22, Ship-yard, and employed by the Bishop's Wharf Shipping Co., sustained serious injuries whilst following his employment on Monday. He was working at the wharf when two bags of barley which were being loaded fell upon him. He was conveyed in the horse ambulance to the Infirmary by P.C.lfuskett,where it was found that both thigh bones were broken and that he had a contused wound on the chest. He was detained in the institution. Drowning Fatality at Sankey. -'

Mr. Briihouse, held an inquest at Warrington on Tuesday on the body of Herbert Stewart (6), who was drowned in the Sankey and St. Helens Canal, at Buttermilk Bridge, on Saturday. Sidney Ormson, ten years of age, son of Richard Ormson, residing at 263, Liverpool-road, said that at eleven o'clock on Saturday morning, in company with deceased and another boy,he went to Buttermilk Swing Bridge. Whilst there they saw a small wooden box floating in the water and deceased went down the bank to reach it.ln doing so he over balanced and fell into the water. Before witness could reach him he sank. Witcess raised an alarm, and a man named Kirk came and recovered the body. The jury returned a verdict of " Accidental death." LAWN TIZINTS, CROQUET, and other outdoor sports.—J. S. GARLAND, 51,Bridge-st.—Advt.

Garman Gipsies at Warrington. The band of German gipsies who for some time have been travelling about the country again visited Warrington on Monday evening. They were escorted to the town by the Cheshire ' police, but were not allowed to remain, being conducted in the direction of Liverpool by members of the Borough force. On arrival at Penketh they were met by the local officer who moved them on to Lindley Green, Great Sankey, where they were allowed to camp for the night, their presende attracting a large crowd of curious spectators. The unwelcome visitors on Tuesday, instead of going on to Liverpool, returned to Warrington. Inspector Taylor and a number of policemen escorted them through the borough again as far as Longford, and then handed them over to the care of the county police. The g,ipsies expressed their intention to make their way to Wigan and Preston. The Late Mr. Charles's Work.

Commenting on the Liverpool Art Exhibition Mr. James Greig, R.8.A., says :—" In Room VIII. there is a painting lln Harvest Time '(876), which has a sad history attached to it. Since it was sent in the artist, Mr. James Charles has died and left a widow and ten children. Mr. Charles, a Warrington man, was one of the most inspiriting artists of our time. Though little known to the general public, his work was highly appreciated by his fellow craftsmen and connoissienra. His pictures were always fresh in thought and point of view, they had technical qualities of a distinct character, and his art fully deserved the high praise passed on them by Mr. H. H. La Thangue, A.R.A ,in a letter to the Times.' The painting on view at Liverpool is admirable in every way. It is finely composed, fresh in colour, and it has a feeling of autumn sunshine and atmosphere. The Corpora- tion of Liverpool or Manchester should secure this excellent example of the painter's style."
