Mrs. Elizabeth Rylands, of Highfields, Thelvrall, who died on the 16th June last, aged 75 years, widow of Mr. Thomas Glazebrook Rylands, left estate valued at £8,689 gross with net personalty £8,414. Probate of her will, which bears date the 7th January, 1903, has been granted to her stepsons, Mr. J. P. Rylands, of 2, Charlesville, Birkenhead, barrister, and Mr. W. W. H. Rylands, of Campden Hill-place, London, and Mr. J. C. Bromfield, of Fenwick-street, Liverpool, solicitor, and her daughter, bliss Martha Glazebrook Rylands. Mrs. Rylands left the funds over which she had power of appointment under the will of her late husband to follow the trusts of her late husband's residuary estate, and her household and personal effects, subject to some specific bequests, she left to her daughter. The residue of her estate, subject to other provisions, she left in trust for her daughter Martha Glozebrook- for life;' and, subject to her interest, she left the ultimate residue of her estate to follow the trusts of her husband's residuary estate. PREMIER CYCLES, 7,8, 10, 12 and 14 guineas Cash or 18 monthly payments. Sole agent, J. S. GARLAND, 51, 53 a 44,55, Bridge-fat.—Advt. Labourer's Singular Death. Mr. Brighouse, coroner, held an inquest at Warring- ton on Tuesday on the body of Joseph Langley (52), 34, Generailtreet, who died on Saturday. Walter Langley genral labourer said deceased had been unwell for three months, but he had not been under medical treatment. On Sunday, the 2nd inst, .the deceased with two other men, went to Glazebury and put up at an hotel for refreshments, which consisted of rabbit pie. On the following morning deceased I resumed work as usual, and all went well until the afternoon, when he suffered from diarrhcsa. On the I following day Dr. E. J. Fox was called in and attended him until he died at 4.40 on Saturday morning. Dr. Fox stated that when he first saw the deceased he was delirious with an irregular pulse, and he formed the opinion that he was suffering from poisoning by some bad food. He had since made a post-mortem examina- tion of the body and had found that death was due to heart failure. There was evidence of inflammation of the stomach, but it was of old standing. The jury returned a verdict in accordance with the medical evidence.

Mr. A. J. King, M.P. and Socialism. The Young British Liberals' Society for Stoekport and the district held its first social gathering at Knutsford on Saturday. Mr. A. J. King, M.P., whose victory over Mr. Alan de Tattoo Egerton at the last election was one of the most significant features which attended the remarkable series of Liberal victories in Cheshire, was the chief speaker. The great questions before the Liberal party to-day, he said, were social questions—questions dealing with the condition of the people, and it was for Liberals to show that they had some remedy, some policy, which would deal with those questions, apart from the extreme Socialism that was advocated by .certain sections of political thought. We were all Socialists to a certain extent. Our factory legislation, the municipal ownership of gasworks and tramways were forms of Socialism, and it was only a question of how far the principle could be carried. Liberals must make up their minds, as Liberals, how far they would go, and upon what principle they would limit it. Mr. King expressed the view that the question of land nationalisationywas at the present moment outside practical politics. What was needed at present,he said, was easier access to the land,and some alteration of the law which would enable municipal corporations to acquire land for the public benefit.

PUBLIC BENEFTT, UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt. Shop Assistants' Meeting. A meeting in connection with the Warrington Branch of the Union of Shop Assistants was held in Messrs. Toft's Assembly Room on Tuesday evening, at which there was a fair attendance. Councillor Ball, who presided, urged that the shop assistants of Warrington lacked boldness and were wanting in a progressive spirit. If they brought themselves mote to the front they would enlist a greater measure of public sympathy and support! (Hear, hear.) Mr. E. Plinston moved " That this meeting claims that all shop assistants whether employed in large or small establishments should be included in the provisions of the Workmen's Compensation Bill and in order that the benefits should not he illusory asks that the Govern- ment should insert a clause in the act for compulsory insurance on the lines of the resolution adopted by the Trades Union Congress; this meeting further believes that reform will come through organization and there- fore strongly urges all shopworkers to become mem- bers of the National Union." Mr. H. Brad: burn, chairman of the Warrington Branch; seconded. In supporting, Mr. W. C. Anderson, organizing secretary of the Union, said that in Warrington he noticed a tendency for shops to be kept open late, and in any cases the hours of closing were far too late. This he thought established beyond doubt the failure of permissive legislation and showed the urgent need for compulsory enactments. Referring to the Compensation Bill he said Mr. Gladstone had acknowledged the necessity of shop assistants being brought within the provisions of the Bill. He urged, however, that in small shops the employer would not be able to pay the compensation and would have to become bankrupt and therefore did not include assistants employed in small shops. He (the speaker) failed to see why one class of assistant should be denied the benefits which another class received, and maintained that all could be put on an equal footing by the Government providing for com- pulsory insurance. (Hear, hear.) Votes of thanks to the speakers concluded the meeting.

WARRINGTON MUSICAL SOCIETY.

Annual Meeting.

The Warrington Musical Society commenced the season's work on Tuesday evening when the first rehearsal was held in the Cairo-street schoolroom. At the close a large number of members remained for the annual meeting which took place under the presi- dency of Mr. F. Monks. The attendance included Mr. L. W. Reynolds (hon. treasurer), Mr. C. P. Gough (hon. secretary) and Mr. Frank H. Crossley (conductor).

THE FINANCIAL POSITION. LOSS ON THE CONCERTS.

Mr. REYNOLDS presented the financial statement and observed that last season's results were disastrous. They were worse than the previous year; in fact the seasons seemed to be getting worse and worse. As the opera became more successful, the concerts were more and more it failure and he was inclined to think that the opera was taking the wind out of the sails of the society's legitimate work. The accounts were in the main satisfactory, so they must consider themselves fortunate The loss was not due to increased expendi- ture or extravagrance, because the expenditure for the season was only about £1 in advance of the previous year. The principal cause of the increased deficiency was a falling off in the subscriptions and in the sale of tickets. The subscriptions totalled £lOB as compared with £122 and the sale of tickets realized £52 4s. as against £Bl. However, with the profit from the opera the society had a balance to the good of £l3l 9s. id. The balance sheet showed that the loss on the concerts was .£B9 ss. 10d. The receipts included £lOB 2s subscriptions to concerts and £4B ss. subscriptions of members. On the expenditure side the chief item was £192 7s. for piofessional aid at concerts. The profit on the performances of "The Mikado" amounted to £214 15s. 9d. After paying all expenses the cash in the bank totalled £137 19s. id. In addition the sum of £2OO was invested in Warrington Corporation bonds. ' Mr. L. W. REYNOLDS moved the adoption of the accounts. Mr. NEWCOMBE seconded.

The CnA.in.ms:s observed that he did not think the society existed so much to make a profit as for the musical education of the people. He did not think the position was such as to cause much anxiety. The motion was put and carried unanimously. THE SEASON'S WORK.

The HON. SECRETARY reported that the usual three subscription concerts were given during the season u follows :—First, Elgar's "King Olaf '" ; second, Stan- ford's" ?avenge " ; third, miscellaneous. For the last named concert all the vocalists were chosen from mem- bers of the society, while the solo violinist was Mr. Louis Wolff. During the season the rehearsals numbered twenty-five in all. At nineteen of these the band and chorus were together and on six occasions on separate nights. The attendance at rehearsals was, on the whole, very satisfactory, and compared favourably with pre- vious years. The opera section of the society was again most remarkably successful in the performances of "The Mikado " in October, and the committee were once more conscious of the debt of gratitude to the performers, the conductor, opera, and all concerned, for the invaluable help given to the funds of the society by their work. Had it not been for the gratifying financial result of the week's performances the Treasurer's report would indeed have been a sorry one for the season's work. The books showed an increase of eight members on the previous year. The chorus consisted of the following :—Sopranos. 68 (two more than last year) ; contraltos, 46 (three more) ; tenors, 22 (one less) ; bassas, 39 (two more)—total, 175. The orchestra was composed of 35 members representing an increase of two. An interesting departure had been made in the presentation by the Society of a collection of musical works to be placed in the Municipal Museum. These would be available for the use of local musical organizations and a catalogue had been issued. He Ndould like to offer his sincere thanks to the con- ductor, the committee, and all the official and non- official members of the society for the constant and kindly help he had always met with in carrying out his pleasant duties. (Applause.) He moved the adoption lof the report. Mr. W. E. ATKIN seconded, and the resolution was agreed to.

OFFICERS.

The following officers were re-elected for the ensu- ing season :—President, Mr. J. Charlton Parr; vice- presidents. the Mayor, Colonel Ridgway, Messrs. Ziba Armitage, W. E. Atkin, A. H. Crosfield M.P., James Fairclough, John Fairclough, R. T. Fairclough, F. A. Frost, W. N. Hutchings, F. Monks, James Munro, C. E. Parker, R. C. Parr, Robert Pierpoint, L. W. Reynolds, H. Roberts and John White; conductor, Mr. F. H. Crossley; hon. organist and accompanist, Mr. W. H. Payton ; leader of orchestra. Mr. R. El. Pearson ' • hon. librarian, Mr. G. Johnson ; hon. registrar, Mr. J. New- combe • lion, treasurer, Mr. L. W. Reynolds; hon. secre- tary, Mr.C. P. Gough; committee: Messrs. H.Bond, J. W. Bibby, G. Johnson, J. T. Knowles, J Newcombe, P.C. Price, W. Scotson, R. H. Silcock, W. H. Walling- ton. and R. Whitfield.

THE PROGRAMME FOR THE SEASON.

EFFORTS ON BEHALF OF THE INFER.VIARY.

Mr. CROSSLEY extended a hearty welcome to the many new members who had joined the society, but said ' be would like to see a few more altos. Arrangements had been made to hold three concerts, and the first two only would be on their own account—" The Crusaders" and miscellaneous on November 7th and "The Messiah" on December 22nd. (Applause.) He hoped they would have a really good performance of" The Messiah" and judging from the way in which the chorus was turn- ing up he thought it would be the best they ever gave. They had engaged an excellent , quartette of vocalists for "The Messiah" concert including Miss Gleason White (soprano), Hr. Charles Saunders (tenor) and Mr. Robert Radford (bass). At the first concert the bari- tone soloist would be Mr. Fred Austin and the tenor Mr. J. Davies. The committee had decided that the third concert' should be given for the benefit of the Infirmary Extension Fund. (Applause.) It would be. a popular concert aud he hoped sustained as much as possible by the members themselves. (Hear, hear.) IThe date fixed was February 9th, which was the Satur- day before Lent. As the members of the society were aware, the proceeds of two nights of the forthcoming opera would also be devoted to the Infirmary Extension. Fund. He hoped they would be enabled to hand over a nice little sum to that deserving object. (Hear, hear.) The season'would end extremely early but he hoped the society would not break up altogether from February 9th until the following season. It bad always proved a loss to give a concert during Lent but after Lent he wanted them to begin to familiarise themselves with any difficult work which was selected ,so that they would be in a position to take it up seriously for the first concert of the season. A vote of thanks to the Chairman concluded the meeting.

The New County Court Judge.

Mr. Shiress Will, K.C., -has been appointed to the vacant county court judgeship filled by the late Mr. Bowen Rowlands, K.C. The new judge is in his sixty-sixth year, has been at the bar for forty-two years, and tolk silk in 1883. He is a Bencher of the Sfiddle Temple. He was returned as the representa- tive of the Montrose Burghs in 1885, and held the seat for nearly eleven years, retiring in Pebrttary, 1896, to give Mr. John Morley a seat. Mr. Will's practice has in recent years lain mainly in Parlia- mentary Committees, where he has held a prominent j though not a foremost position. It comes rather as a Isurprise that he should be willing to accept a county court judgsbip, where the emoluments by no means equal those which fall to the lot of a practitioner before the House of Commons Committees. It is most unusual for a Parliamentary counsel to become a county court judge, but Mr. Will's long and wide experience is a guarantee that the duties of his new position will be adequately performed.

President Roosevelt has extended the eight hour law to make it apply to all public work carried out under the supervision of any Goverment Department. Dinners at an Edinburgh hotel were alarmed by a revolver shot. Blood was found trickling from under a lavatory door, and a man, said to be an army officer, was found inside with his brains blown out. Customs Dock No. 4, at Buenos Ayres, containing a quantity of European merchandise, has been destroyed by fire. The damage is estimated at several millions of paper dollars. The shipping is safe

!EP INTO' BABYLIND.

_ It was. a lovely summer's afternoon, and the sun was shining brilliantly. On the iwii of a beautiful country house a pretty little g.rt was Sitting. She felt very lonely the hot atter- noon, for she had no brothers nor iiiste:s te play with her. At Last elte got up and started for a walk. Little Lottie, for that was her name, went on and on in the scorching heat, until she came to a large wood. She at down under a large shady oak tree, and fell f abt asleep._ _ _

"Suddenly she awoke; but were was she? Ia a lovely garden, with great, tall flowers.; but what made her look so astonished? Why I peep- ing out _from a dainty' pink rose was a sweet little curly-headed baby girl, with a saucy smile on. her little face. Lottie went over to the baby, and was looking at her intently, w.lierc a kind-faced lady came up and said, "14 hat do you think of my babies, Lottie." "Oh, dear ady, I do love them, and will you plsase let take home a little girl to play with me, ;ause lam so lonely. I have no brothers nor isters, like other little girls." " Yes, clear Lottie, you shall choose klitle sister. yourself."

r. our little Lottie could ,not speak' for joy, but the lady led her all round the garden, and let her peep into every flower; but, although she saw so many babies she told the lady that the one she liked best was in the •big pink rue. The pretty lady then went over to the rose, and said, in a soft, cooing tone, "Rosie, Rosie, your sister has come to fetch you." The little one lifted up her arms, and the lady was just placing her in Lottie's arms, when she awbke with a start. Poor Lottie, imagine her dismay when she found that she was sieterlese once more. "A dream, a dream," she sobbed, as she ran home. But when she saw her papa. coming to meet her, she stopped crying. Her papa lifted her into his strong arms, an carried her into mamma's room, and, without a, word he lifted back the curtains of a dainty little cot, and there lay a sweet little baby-girl. • Oh, my dear, darling Rosie, my little flower baby, the Baby Queen has sent you. I shall never be lonely any more.

MY t)EAR CEILDRBN,-

This week I am able to announce a new Competition for girls and boys also, and I hope that you will take great interest, and all join. You will see the first letter I have printed is from Herbert King, and I think his sfiggestion for a Competition for boys' a very good one, and co I _shall adopt it. Any member may join, and you can all choose your own subject for a drawing or painting. Neatness will be taken Into consideration, and I hope yOu will all keep these drawings as clean as potssible. I should advise mounting them on a piece of cardboard. TWo prizes will be awarded, and both are well worth tying to gain. All those competing must send me the drawings by the Bth of October, addressed to "Uncle Ned," office of :this paper.

As to the Competition for the girls' i find that you are all very fond of any kind of needlework, so I propose that you should all make some small fancy article for "Engle Ned." Many of you have asked me what I would like as a small present, so here is an .opportunity for you all to make me something. I propose such things as fancy mats, doyleys, cravat cases, Or ANY OTHER thing that you may think of. I have THREE lovely prizes waiting for the one who sends the prettiest and best- made article. I hope every one of you will join this Competition. It is easier than any other I have given, as you can make whatever you like, and, naturally,, you will select the work•that you car, do the best. You all stand equal chances, so why not. try and gain a beautiful prize? All the girls must send me these articles by October 15th. This gives you plenty of time, and do not forget to address the parcel to "UNCLE NED," office of this paper. You will see we have several new members this week, and all are very welcome. I' hope they will try and gain prizes in the above-men- tioned competitions. I have sent prizes this week to Winifred Elliott and Elsie Lewis. The latter's contri- bution is original, and, I think, well deserves a nice box of Fry's Chocolates. must not take up more space now, or there will be no room left to print all your interesting contributions.

With all good wishes, from lINcLE NED.

PUSSY AND THE BALL.

A. pretty little kitten, full of liveliness and glee, Was sitting on a window-sill, intently watch-

ing me, Because there dangled from my hand a balkof

wool so red, And every now and then it tapped the kitten

on the lead. I placed the ball upon my head, and opened up my book. Still pussy sat with folded paws, and quite a

sleepy look, Till suddenly she sprang upon my shoulder

with a bound, - And pussy, with the ball of wool, came tumbling to the ground. From your loving Niece, DOROTHY SIMPSON.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l have not written to you 'for quite a long time, but I hoe you are quite well. We have been having very hot weather just lately. I went to the Rosherville Gardens on Saturday. It was very nice among the flower-beds, when they were all lighted up. They have some very daring acrobats there. I have been thinking about a competition. for boys. How would it do to have a drawing competition, and, perhaps, paintings of flowers, landscape, animals, or children? I think most boys can paint and draw a little now, and it would be something for dark evenings, which are coming...fast. I hope all the coulins have enjoyed their holidays. Thanking you for the bottle of Mason's Extract of Herbs, I must now close with all good. wishes. I am your loving Nephew,' HERBERT KING.

As be robed himself in his great Lvthate cloak, And I must move, ere the daylight comes, And get those apples—they are /ove/y ones." Then he smacked his lips, as Jae thought of the

feast He would, have in the barn, with the light from

the East. So he went salort the silent roads

Till he came to' the orchard of Farmer Rhodes. Tiien he climbed up the tree, and heard a growl, " What can it he 1" said the Ghost, " p'raps 'tis an owl."

"THE TRIO."

Three little maids from school, Each ,stood on a nice little stool, So people could see, ohl And then sang a trio— Three little Maid out of school. A new hat they each of them wore, With strings hanging right down before. 'They sang so discreetly, And neatly and sweetly, That everyone wanted some more. 30 Liverpool City

COMMERCIIL FArcurtns.—According to " Kemp's Mercantile Gazette," the total number of Commercial failures recorded in England and Wales during the week ending Friday, September 14th, was 137, viz. : New Bankruptcy Proceedings published in the London Gazette, 63; Deeds of Arrangement registered,69. The respective numbers in the corresponding week of last year were : Bankruptcies, 78 ; Deeds of Arrangement 93—total 141; being a decrease of 4. The total num- ber of Commercial Failures recorded during the 37 weeks of the present year was 5,935; the total number recorded in the corresponding 37 weeks of last year was 6,314, showing is decrease of 379.

During the septennial beating of the bounds at Richmond, Yorkshire on Wednesday, a monument was unveiled to commemorate " Willance's Leap'" of 200 feet over a cliff 300 years ago, when his horse was killed, but the rider escaped with a broken leg.

RESULTS OF SATURDAY'S GAMES.

G. T. P. , G. T. P Salford $ 4 22 Leeds Hunslet Warrington Halifax 4 6 26 Broughton R. ... 4 3 17 Huddersfield Oldham Keighley Barrow Dewsbury - Hull

Rochdale H.. Runcorn York

Warrington Win at St-. Helens.

Five or six hundred Wdiringtonians accompanied the team to St. Helens on Saturday, and had the satisfac- tion of seeing .a handsome win recorded. Even at St. Helens, Wn arngton were expected to win and their chances were increased by the absence of Dal Harris from the home three-quarter line. There was a crowd of quite 3,000, which is much above the average for the glass blowing-centre. Teams: St. Helens—Atkinson, back ;Prescott, Hillen, Bocock and Bates, three-quarter backs ; Briers and Toole, half backs; Mavity, Mooney, Whiteley, Pope, Dolan, and Smith, forwards.

Warrington—Berry, back; Fish, Isherwood, Taylor, and Brooks, three-quarter backs ; Hockenhall and Lees, half backs ; Preston, Boardman, Heath,Harmer, Thomas and Naylor, forwards. Referee, Mr. H. Edmundson, Bradford. Touch judges, Messrs. E. Bennett, Liverpool and W. Gregory, Runcorn. Play opened at a fast pace and Berry was soon called upon. He missed his catch and the home side narrowly missed taking the ball over. Warrington quickly got on the offensive, and a fine dri,bble by Harmer tested Atkinson fully. Play settled in the home quarter, and a brilliant round of passing by Hockenhall, Isherwood, Taylor, and Brooks resulted in a smart try to the last named, which Preston converted from a difficult angle. Warrington kept up a hot attack and Preston kicked a ' penalty goal, but his own forwards got offside and nulli- fied the effort. The Saints got little respite, however, and after a fine rush by the Warrington forwards Preston threw to Fish who eluded his opponents and scored. Preston failed at goal. The home side now made a great effort and Fish was collared with the ball near the line. He threw, away and Toole secured, the ball being punted into the Warrington goal. A try seemed certain when Brooks dashed up and made a daring save. Hockenhall then got away with a fine effort and passing to Isherwood, the latter made an opening for Fish which was brilliantly taken advantage of, Preston fail- ing to improve. The homesters again rushed the ball to the other end, and Briers scored under the bar through charging Lees' kick. Toole landed a goal. Half-time score:—Warrington 1 goal 3 tries (11 points), St. Helens 1 goal 1 try (5 points). Preston started the second portion, and after some kicking exchanges Prescott dashe(kto the line, but was bowled into touch by Lees and.,Berry, who were injured in tackling him, Berry having to retire. Briers next dribbled over the crease, but missed the leather. St. Helens penned Warrington in close quarters,and Mavity scored under the posts, Toole kicking a goal. Play became very excituadbut when Berry returned Fish placed his side further ahead with a try behind the post, he also kicking the goal. On resuming, Isherwood stole away, and feinting to pass, scored at the corner, the place kick failing. St. Helens cobtinued to press and had hard lines just as the whistle went. Result Warrington 2 goals 5 tries (19 points), St. Helens 2 goals 2 tries (10 points). RUNCORN AVENGED ON WIDNES.

The return rcatch between Runcorn and Widnes was played on Saturday at Runcorn, when the Linnets were triumphant in the presence of 3,000 people. G. Aspey started for Widnes, and following a good return by Watton, the Widnes forwards dribbled to the home line, Jolley eventually relieving. From another dribble Flanagan smartly got possession, and scored a try,which Jolley failed to improve. On resuming, Jolley, Butter- worth, Watton, Moran, and Flanagan gave a fine display of passing, the latter player scoring, Jolley again failing at goal. Haidly had the game restarted when Flanagan dashed over the line, for ,Tolley to add a goal. Noon and Taylor got into the Runcorn half, brit Harrison regained the lost ground. Widnes were penalised, Richardson placing a goal for Runcorn. Watton received a nasty kick, and was carried off the field. A. Taylor kicked a goal, and then Padbury dashed through the Widnes backs in fine style, scoring under the posts. Jolley added a goal, and also placed a penalty goal. Half-time score :—Runcorn 4 goals and 4 tries (20 points), Widnes 1 goal (2 points). After the interval passing between Watton, Butter- worth, Moran, and Flanagan gained ground for Runcorn, but the visiting forwards dribbled back to the home half, where Taylor placed a penalty goal for Widnes, and also dropped a goal. Near the olose Moran scored a try, but Jolley failed at goal. Result :—Runcorn 4 goals 5 tries (23 points), Widnes 3 goals (6 points). Teams:—

Runcorn—Harrison, back; Deakin' Butterworth, Moran and Flanagan, three-quarter backs ; Jolley and ViTatton, half backs ; Walker' Richardson, Padbury, Leyland, Kennedy, and Dixon forwards. Widnes—Tilley, back ; Barber, Davies, Taylor and Ditchileld, three-quarter backs; Noon and Pennington, half backs; G. Aspey, S. Aspey, Argent, Carr, Rigby and Turner, forwards.

Referee, Mr. A. Smith, Halifax. Touch judges Messrs. A. Jones, Warrington, and W. N evins, Warrington.

===== 0002601_19060922_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON " A.'S " EASY VICTORY.

Warrington" A "had quite a field day on Saturday when they entertained St. Helens " A " and defeated them by 49 points to nil. About 2,500 spectators assem- bled to witness the match. Operations had not been long in progress before the forwards rushed the ball to the line and Foster got over. St. Helens attacked but were easily repelled by Knight and a fine passing move- ment ended in Harris receiving from Dickenson and scoring in grand style. A few minutes later Jordan distinguished himself by scoring behind the posts after a brilliant run. Warrington carried all before them and play settled near the visitors' line, Landor eventu- ally dashing over. Just before half time Harris received the ball from a scrimmage near the half way line and had an easy task in scoring. Half time, War- rington one goal five tries (17 points) ; St. Helens nil. On resuming Warrington worked with a will and the visitors had never the slightest chance. Tries were scored byrartwright, Lunt, Davies, Harris, Dickenson, and Landor, four goals being placed by Davies. Result : Warrington, 5 goals, 13 We:, 49 points ; St. Helens, nil.

HOUSEKEEPING ADVICE FROM THE KING'S PHYSICIAN. Sir Francis Lakinff (the King's physician), expresses the opinion that dried currants are so full of brain and body building properties that they should be used daily. Currants are also chief amongst the more valuable foods at men's command, as they contain so much grape sugar as to be all actual nutriment, and when Introduced into bread form, an all sufficient qrticle of diet.

Municipal Elections.

Sir.—ls it not time that our leaders took steps to strengthen the party in the Town Council? Suitable Liberal candidates could easily win St. Austin's and Fair- field Wards,whilst I think witha little effort Orford and Town Hall Wards might also be secured to the party. The general election showed what we could do if we liked and I hope on theist of November we may reduce the number of Tories in our local parliament.—Yours respectfully, Liverpool-road, Sept. 20th, 1906.

DAVID COOPER.

===== 0002601_19060922_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
Family Gardens.

Sir,-13nder this head great progress has been made in the last ten years in German towns, with immense benefit to the financial, spiritual and moral welfare of the working classes. In one respect Germany possessed an enormous advantage, namely in her common lands. Of these 1,500 German towns and villages own enough to pay all Imperial and local rates and taxes. In 500 of them there is even an excess of revenue so large indeed that they are able to pay every citizen on New Year's day from £5 to £2O as his share out of the common revenue. In some cases this common land being in or near a town is divided into plots of one tenth of an acre, and these are rented at from 10s. to 15s. a plot. Such a plot is big enough to grow nearly all the vegetables required by an average family of five. Moreover the family has the advantage of recreative, wholesome work, in good fresh air after a day spent in the smoky town and its narrow streets. Why should we not have the same advantage in our towns? If it is difficult to find a landowner in Warrington who will sell the land for such a purpose at a reasonable price why not start on the few acres of common land owned by Stockton Heath. The few grounds it yielded would be used for the relief of the poor, thus demonstrating the double advantage of the scheme. Perhaps then the Corporation of Warrington would force some landowner to part with " his " land, which he never has made, at a reasonable price for the welfare of the great majority of the town population. I shall be very pleased to supply any further information on this subject and related questions.--Yours &c., B. OCKEL, 9, Walton New-road.

===== 0002601_19060922_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
"The Amalgamation of Labour."

• f Sir,— A question o extreme importance is about to be submitted in local Trade Union circles, the answer- ing of which will give us some idea as to the amount of progress made in the trade union movement during the last eventful twelve months. I need scarcely say that I am referring specially to question No. 1 of the series of 3, because, personally, I believe questions No. 2 and 3 are entirely retrogressive, and the best policy would have been to have omitted them altogether. Their utter want of toleration and political foresight are reminiscent of 19th century bogies. The L.R.C. represents the high water mark of labour attainment so far, and if it has not been as successful as many of us desire, it has not been on account of its constitution—which is on abroad basis—because the Socialists left it nearly two years ago, in order to see ifiit was they who were the cause of its comparative non-success. No, its want of entire success must be attributed unfortunately to the apathy and indifference displayed by the majority of trade union branches and not to any fault of its constitution. But we may hope that many branches will by this time have seen the wisdom of reversing their former policy with regard to political action. Whatever the verdict may be, we cannot afford to go back on the lines suggested by (ques- tions No. 2 and 3. To say the least of them, it looks like complete desertion of the L.R.C. position, and an attempt to farm the labour movement by trade unionists only, and I need scarcely say that such a step would from the very first be doomed to failure, and would be an entire waste of time and opportunity. Let us have a plain answer to a plaia issue. My object in writing is to plead the cause of unity, which in plain terms, carries with it the right of the Socialist to join hands with the trade unionist in the formation of a political party of their own, which will take an all- round interest in the welfare of the workers from an industrial and social point of view as well as politically. At the outset let me say emphatically that unity will not and cannot weaken trade unionism, for the simple reason that it will not interfere with the autonomy of the branches ; on the contrary, united action is always more powerful and more successful than by units. Secondly, the position of trade unionism would always be a strong one, for assuming the I.L.P. to be the only society likely to be outside trade unionism, the voting power of the latter would be 98 per cent., as against two per cent, of the former. True, it may be said that Socialists would creep in through their branches. Just so, but whose fault would that be? Not the Socialists. It seems to me that the trade unionists would have a very strong position indeed if they chose to exercise it, and need have no fear of Socialism. But why fear Socialism at all? No proof is needed when I say the Socialist is always a good trade unionist, and if we take the membership of the I.L.P. we shall find that 95 per cent, are trade unionists and of the very best too. Then again, the three councillors who look after the interests of trade unionists on the Town Council are all Socialists, and many of the most prominent officers of the trade union movement are also Socialists. That these men are a source of strength, goes without saying. Consequently I fail to see any logic or commonsense in any other answer being given than a manly " Yes," to question No I. In conclusion, let me urge upon trade unionists to think deeply and earnestly on this matter. Above all. let them think of the enormous income tax some of them are paying by way of contributions, and ask themselves if there is no remedy, no better and saner way of attaining their ends. I urge them to cic• their duty to themselves, to their children and posterity, and by so doing, earn for themselves a page in the history of trade unionism. Lastly, if, as I think, from want of knowledge they are not able to see eye to eye with the Socialists, let them not forget that Socialism is coming and that it is a great and worthy cause, and that the men who already believe in it heart and soul are worthy of trust.—Yours truly, DELEGATE.

===== 0002601_19060922_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. Morton and his Critics.

Sir,—The letter of" Gasworker in your last week's issue put in a very forcible way, a very common but very erroneous I.L.P. idea of the relation between Labour Representation and Liberalism and I shall have pleasure in putting the matter in what we consider its true light. I am sure " Gasworker " will agree with me that to distort facts and to make the truth appear false is not the way to secure reforms and the better- mett of the conditions of the working classes at which we both profess to be aiming. That I don't come with with a tomahawk is not to be taken as an indication of the true merits of the case and with regard to his query " Which are our labour organisations ?" this is plainly a superfluous question, for no one knows the labour organisations better than the member of the I.L.P. seeing that his party feeds its a parasite on them and would without them have little vitality. But now to the main question. What is the theory of Labour Representation and how is it that all Lsbour M.P.'s turn out to be Liberals? "Gasworker " has given a list of Labour members who are not ashamed to be called Liberal and if it does nothing else it is sufficient to prove that Labour Representation was an accom- . plished fact before the I.L.P. appeared, and that this is therefore but a plant borrowed from Liberalism. We have in the case of a mining district where practic- ally the whole of the population are miners conditions conducive to the simplest and most direct form of labour representation possible. There is one Paramount union which has the power to send to Parliament its own paid agent. The members of the union may be Liberal and Conservative and the miners' agent himself may have professed to be a Conservative—but the ques- tion of party politics is put aside and the miners send their own paid official to look after their own trade Interests without financial assistance from any Liberal or Conservative organization. Could there he a purer form of labour representation than this? Now what becomes of this labour representative when he gets to Parliament? From the oratory of the rank and file of the I.L.P. he would be met on the steps of St. Stephen's by the leaders of both parties with open arms, each eager to enlist him in their service ; but to these, being a labour man, he replies," I am having none of you— seri motto is Down with everything." This is the viocitrine sedulously taught by the rank and file of the LLlP—that both parties are equally had and both opposed to the interests of the workers. With the leaders, how- ewer, the attitude is.different. They know that such a course is impossible, that it could only result in the breakup of their party. Before the general election, as I have before pointed out, Mr- Ramsey Macdonald indicated very clearly in the" Speaker "the course the Independent Labour section would have to pursue, i e it would "adopt an attitude from session to session,voting sometimes for or against minor matters to which it is opposed or of which it is in favour in order that it may prevent the defeat of a government which on the whole at desires to keep in power." This is exactly what our isolated miners' representative would do and accounts for the fact that he Is always found supporting the Liberal party because through it alone he can expect the reforms demanded by hie union. He need not be con- nected with any Liberal organisation to bea Liberal, be can be as independent Os the most extreme I.L.P'er could wish ; hut, unless he is going to waste his parlia- mentary time he would have to make the best of the circumstances in which he is placed and co-operate with any force he finds making for a common end, and as such co-operation must necessarily be given to the party of Reform be is classed as a Liberal. Can " Gasworker give me any valid reason why the so called Independent Lebow section should not also be classed as Liberals ? Mr. Maddison in the course of an interview reported in the "Tribune " of September 6th says "I am just as

independent as the richest man in the House. Ido not ! receive, and never have received, a single penny of salary from the Liberal Party." The Labour members ! who own their Libralismare as free to act in a group and ! shape the policy of the Liberal party as though they belonged to an any so called independent party. With regard to Mr. Hampson's effulusion, I think I need not say very much as he has cE,rtainly misread my letter—whetherintentionally or not is not for me to say—but if matters of public interest are to be properly discussed those engaged in them should at least be able to grasp the meaning of ordinary language. I was quite ignorant before his letter appeared whether Mr. Hampson had reached that stage of education where a man begins to realize now little he knows and is seek- ing modestly for truth or whether he had only arrived at that age when he knows everything; but now I know and note,that he even deems himself capable of sustain- j ing the role of a dictator but very kindly refrains from ! exercising this interesting function out of respect to , myself. This is very kind indeed and I can assure him that I shall always feel for him the warmest apprecia- tion that is possible to one who is evidently up in a I balloon." Mr. Hampson says that my version of the evolution of the "Right to Work Committee" ! is not quite correct but does not say where. He practically endorses what I said. What he ' shut his eyes to is the "newfangled " (I repeat the , word—it can be found in the dictionary and conveys the sense of" novelty ") nature of the committee. I , know of course that some unions are in theory repre- sented through the Trades Council and some through the L.R.C. but if this irregular way of constituting the com- mittee had not been resorted to the I.L.P. could not have been brought in as an organisation. The irregu- larity would have appeared clear as daylight to Mr. Hampson and his friends if the unions had nominated as the three organisations,the Trades Council,the L.R.C. and the Warrington Liberal Association. This is the course that I said was at variance with the democratic principles guiding true trade unionism ; yet he distorts my meaning and applies it to the ostensible objects of the committee in a way which will appear absurd to your readers and in which I don't feel inclined to follow him. But with regard to the objects of the so-called " !tight to work committee." Mr. Hampson is evidently quite at sea. Its object is not to find work for the unemployed—the leaders of the I.L.P. frankly admit that there is and can be no remedy for unemployment short of" Socialism "and no two of them can agree as to what this is. The " Right to work Committee "is nothing more than a " decoy " intended to draw unthinking people into the Socialist party, an exploita- tion of the unemployed as reprehensible in a political party as it is in a capitalist class. As to organising search parties to find the I.L.P. balloon this was quite unnecessary as everyone who knows anything about the matter knew that it would make its appearance before the November election.—Yours truly, JOHN MORTON, Hon. Sec. Progressive Labour League. Warrington, September 20th, 190 G.

LOCAL WEDDINGS.

Morgan—Norman. A. pretty wedding took place at St. Paul's Church at noon on Wednesday when Miss Bertha Norman, youngest daughter of the late Mr. Ralph Norman (formerly Chief Inspector of Nuisances for the Borough), Bewsey-street, was married to the Rev. J.E. Morgan, 8.A., senior curate of the Parish Church, Llangollen, son of Mr. H. W. Morgan, Capal Bangor, Aberyswith. The ceremony was performed by the Rev. W. Bracecamp in the presence of a large gather- ing. The service was choral "The voice that breathed o'er Eden" being sung whilst appro- priate selections were rendered by Miss Small- wood, who presided at the organ. The bride, who was given away by her brother, Mr. G. T. Norman, Richmond, looked charnAng in a dress of pale cream silk merv, trimmed with brussels lace and ' chiffon, while beneath a tulle veil she wore a wreath of orange blossoms. She also wore a gold curb bracelet and carried a white ivoryrbacked prayer book, the gifts of the bridegroom. The bridesmaids, Miss Laura Norman and Miss Nellie. Taylor, were charmingly attired in pink gowns with overdresses of flowered moussline de poplin trimmed with Irish crochet lace, with sashes of pink ribbon, and hats of white embroidered chiffon with long pink streamers. They also wore, gold signet rings and carried shower bouquet of pink roses,gifts of the bridegroom. After the wedding breakfast, which was held at Laburnum Villa, Froghall- lane, the happy couple left for Llandudno where the honeymoon is being spent. The bride's travelling dress was of violet cloth and she worn a hat to match. The bells at the Parish Church, Llangollen, were rung at intervals during the day in honour of the event.

An interesting wedding was solemnized at St. Benedict's Church on Tuesday when Miss Mary Catherine Byrne, daughter of the late Mr. Richard Byrne, Orford- lane, was married to Mr. Joseph Mark Bate, son of Mr. Peter Bate, Rolleston-street. Billingham—Pickton.

A large congregation assembled at St. Paul's Church on Thursday on the occasion of the marriage of Miss , Harriet Pickton,clanghter of the late Mr. David Pickton, Allen-street, and Mr. Alfred Billinghatn, Kendrick-et. The officiating clergyman was the Rev. W. Bracecamp. The bride, who was given away by her brother, was attired in a beautifully embroidered white dress and wore a veil and wreath of orange blossom. She carried a bouquet of lilies of the valley and wore a gold watch and chain, the gift of the bridegroom. The brides- maids, Miss Lann and Miss G. Lann, Manchester, wore dresses of pale green delaine and hats to match and carried shower bouquets of marguerites. Their orna- ments were gold curb bracelets the gifts of the bride- groom. Mr. Billingham, brother of the bridegroom, acted as best M3O. Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Ban., presided at the organ and rendei-ed appropriate selec- tions. After the wedding breakfast, which was held at the Winmarleigh Cafe, the newly-married couple left for Blackpool for the honeymoon, the bride travelling in a costume of navy blue and a white hat.

(Applause.) 'The Rev. Dr. W. J. TOWNSEND, in the course of a brief but powerful address, said he felt that God was calling the Free Church Council to a tremendous work just now. As he looked around he felt almost appalled at the tremendous and all-important work which they must undertake and dare not shirk. The wree Church Council was doing a work in the nation to-day which no other side of the nation could do—work which if left undone would subject the nation to reaction on every side, and this country would pass into decadence rather than into progress and into comparative darkness rather than into light. With regard to education there were two things that Free Church Councils must at once undertake the responsibility of, in order that they might not be wronged in regard to the Bill now before the House of Lords and in order that they might make the best use of the West Riding Judgment, which had, as he thought, been so Providentially pronounced. They must bring tremendous influence to bear on the County Councils that they might take some such line in their respective areas as the West Riding Council and the Welsh Councils had adopted. Then they must stTengthen the hands of the Government when the Education Bill came from the House of Lords. There was no doubt that the Bill would come forth TREMENDOUSLY MANGLED and the probability was that clause 4 would be made mandatory. FEDERATION OF FREE CHURCHES. CONFERENCES AND PUBLIC MEETING AT WARRINGTON. Visit of the Rev. J. Scott Lidgett.

The South-West Lancashire Federation of Free Church Councils, of which Mr. C.E.Parker,of Warring- ton, is the president, held a series of meetings at Warrington on Thursday, the proceedings having a special interest by reason of the visit of the Rev. J. Scott Lidgett, president of the National Free Church Council, and the Rev. W. J. Townsend, an ex-president of the Council.

In the morning a conference was held in the Church Parlour at Bold-street Wesleyan Church, the principal speaker being the Rev. J. Scott Lidgett. Mr. C. E. Parker presided and amongst those present were : Revs. J. Yonge, S. Pitt, J. Manning, C. L. Stowe, F. Vaughan, E. J. Gawne, J. F. Matthews, and J. S. Hughes (Warrington)' A. J. W. Back (Lymm), G-. W. I Gervis and D. T. Mann (Golborne), Joshua Pedlar, J. W. Hughes, S. J. De!afield, W. J. Baker, J. P. Street and A. Harvey (Widnes), J. Moran Ridge and M. Bosworth (Wigan), Dr. McFarlane' T. Graham, S. R. Bawtree (Southport), W. Holland (St. Helens), and F. T. White (Ashton-in-Makerfield), Messrs. F. Jenkinson (St. Helens), D. Lewis (Widnes), T. Anderton (Wigan), E. England (Warrington) and W. Blight (Wrightington). The CRAIRMA.N on behalf of the Warrington Free Church Council extended a hearty welcome to the visitors from a distance and in the name of the Federa- tion, expressed the pleasure it afforded them to have the presence of the President of the National Council. (Applause). He had been associated with Mr. Scott ' Lidgett for many years in the special work of the Bermondsey settlement and had learned to esteem, honour, and love him. He was very pleased that in the wisdom of their great Council Mr. Scott Lidget was elected to till the honourable position of president. (Applause.) Continuing Mr. Parker said he was sure that gathering would be for good and would enable them as Free Churchmen to enlarge their vision of the possibilities that lay in the federation of Free Churches. (Applause.) The Rev. J. SCOTT LIDGETT, who was heartily received, addressed the meeting on "The immediats work of the Free Church Council." The subject, he said, was so vast that if he were to deal with it exhaus- tively he would cover almost all the necessities, more spiritual or more practical, of the Christian religion throughout the world. First let him refer to the enor- mous responsibility and the great difficulty of the times in which they lived. The world had been drawn so near to them in all its relationships and in all its parts that the responsibility imposed at present upon Christian men was almost indefinitely increased, and with the indefinite increase of their responsibilities in regard to the Kingdom of God at home and abroad there came to them the greatest call for such intense consecration to our Lord Jestti Christ that He might give them that sufficiency which they could only receive from Him. He could not help thinking that the immediate work of the Free Church Council in principle was two-fold—to give practical direction to religion and to give religi- ous inspiration to progress. There was a good deal of wasted energy in the churches at the present time. He was glad that his friend Mr. Jowett had called attention to the effeminacy of a great many conceptions of Christ's religion. He (the speaker) would yield to none in setting forth the aspect of Christianity which regarded it as the education and discipline of the spiritual faculties of life, but above all he was afraid of that abstract conception of spirituality which imagined that perfection could be reached and exercised apart from those great, common, human relationships in which the Kingdom of Chri,st was to be manifested and to'provail. A great many of their greatest difficulties had come from the fact that hitherto spiritual religion hod been nourished apart, instead of finding its manifestation in the great practical life of men. There were too many people who looked upon the churches as

===== 0002601_19060922_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
ECCLESIASTICAL GREENIIOUSES

in which tender plants were placed and for which the pastor and teacher of the church was appointed gently to screen them from the mid-day heat by drawing the blinds,to graduate the temperature week in and week out, to discuss with them the delicate and interesting inner problems of their own life and heart, b,ut to carefully abstain from obtruding upon their notice any of those great and grave social problems by power to deal with which the Christian Church in these days would be judged. The Free Church Council movement had often incurred the hostility of selfish interests azid of this depraved form of spiritual conception by thS courage- ous way in which it had sought to hondle the grave problems of our social life at home and of our imperial policy abroad. If they had not the courage, the faith, and the vitality to grapple with those great problems they were not worth calling into existence. Let them never turn aside from that path because some of their weaker brethren feared where they were going to. If the Free Church Council was worth having, it was called into being to address itself to those great problems which the complex society of the 19th and 20th centuries had seen called into existence, to do something to remedy that NARROW AND SELFISH INDIVIDUALISM of the past which had been content to enjoy spiritual luxuries in churches, feebly, gy the exercise of spasmodic benevolence, to minister to some of the worst results of social injustice and wrong,without ever having the courage, the sacrifice, or the reason to probe deeply the cause of such evils and then in the name of Christ to resist theta. Their first business for which they must run risks and not fear sometimes to alienate either the selfish or the timid must be to give this out- ward direction to the principles of the religion of our Lord. Equally on the other side they must give religious conviction and spiritual inspiration to the cause of progress. He firmly believed that apart from faith in Christ, from continual inspiration from Him,the whole course of mere social amelioration would tend to

.)lATERIALISM AND ENDLESS DISAPPOINTMENT. It could only be by great spiritual forces that the salvation of men would be worked out. The individual could only be reached by great religious inspiration, with all the quickening ideals that it brought, and the only way in which men of goodwill could be rallied and brought in one heart and mini to address them- selves to the great social problems of our time was when they were enabled to rise above the mere animal ' arid the temporal and to look upon man as a spiritual being!whose highest and noblest ideals were to be satisfied and who could only be impelled to the highest and noblest life when his hopes and faith became worthy of the ' name of Chrsit. In this period or sonial reconstruction, when new hopes and ideals were eotn!ng into existence on every hand, they must show a religion which, while it was redeeemed from effeminacy and sentimentality, derived the spring of its hope,. the motive of its self- sacrifice from the supreme, spiritual ideals of Christ. They could not too much lay to heart the fact that at present the Free Churches had an immense amount of practical responsibility laid upon thern,and consequently that which would give them the greatest searching of heart and that by which their practical capacity would be measured would he the p)wer to See exactly the line by which the principle and the practicable were to he brought into union. They had rejoiced that some of them took part some time age in a great meeting at which they celebrated the return of 200 Free Churchmen to Parliament. (Applause.) They cheered that sentiment and so, in a sense, did he. At the same time that meeting was announcing to the world that in a very real sense the Free Church Council had come to power—not that they expected those Free Churchmen who had been elected to treat themselves as the mere delegates of the Free Church Council in Parliament. God forbid that they should ever conceive of their duty as simply composing a kind of spiritual element to a political caucus in the public life of our times. The best homage that they could pay to the character, conscience, and capacity of a

FREE CHURCHMAN INPARLTAMENT was to trust him in the circumstances in which he found himself largely to act according to the principles which they believed his Free Churchmanship had installed into him. At the same time the very fact of the return of 200 Free Churchmen to Parliament had invested the Council with peculiar responsibility in the practical working out, so far as the times were ripe for them, of the great measures of political, social, and moral reform. At the present time one or two of those questions were immediately pressing upon Parliament for solution. He referred particularly to the education controversy and the temperance question. The greatest responsibility which had ever come upon the Free Churches and which perhaps would ever come upon them in the life-time of those present would be in the next few weeks, and they would have to prepare their minds, hearts, and dispositions for that great crisis. In all probability the National Free Church Council would meet in London when

THE Houss OF Loans had done its work of revising, amending, perhaps mutilating the Education Bill in committee. They would then ho called, representing the Free Churchmen of this country. and recruited by representatives from every part of the land, to make a most momentous decision, to stand clear with their prin,ioles, hut to con- sider what were the balanced considerations as to the immediately practicable. They could not possibly accept a measure if the great principles for which they stood and for which the electors of this country had declared, were set ask and rendered futile. At the same time, while they said that distinctly and as much in the cause of peace as with any other object, when they had taken up that firm, clear line marked out for them by duty and conviction they would have to remem- ber history, and the divided condition of men's con- sciences, and to bear in mind the practicable. They would be of all men the most foolish if they were to reject a proposal which gave them in the main public control, freedom of conscience. andlefficiency of educational machinery,because in some respects, perhaps in many, it fell short of the ideal. To decide exactly the point at which that spirit should prevail and lead them to accept not the highest, necessarily, or the ideal

EVENING CONFERENCE.

HOW TO ArraNCT THE VOTJ'NG.

- At the conclusion of tha service tea was served in the schoolroom, after which a tea table conference was held. At the cutest a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to the Warrington Council for thlir hospitality, to the Committee responsible for the arrangements, and to the trustees of the Bold-street Church for the use of the room, on the motion of the Rev. A. HARVEY, seconded by Councillor LEWIS. The Bev. .1. F. MA-VIM:WS briefly responded. The Rev. Dr. TOWNSEND, who had a warm reception, said that the most pressing thing he knew of in the work of the Federation was to capture the young men and young women. He did not think they captured the ' young people as they ought to do, and there. was one ' fact which he thought would show that in a most terrible way. The Sunday School Union showed that they were losing from their Sunday schools 90 percent. of the scholars, and were only saving for the Churches and for Christian life ten per cent. They ought never to rest satisfied until those figures had been reversed. If they had to lose at all, let them not lose more than the ten per cent. He thought the Federation could do a great deal to resist that waste, and to gather it up to be used mightily in England for the Free Churches and for the glory of God. They could try to do something for the young women, especially those of the poorer classes, and while he was not aware that that matter had been takcin up with any degree of energy in that district he was thank fat to say that in some districts and in many of their larger cities the girl's guild was doing a truly magnifi- cent work. An enormous amount of work had been done by the Church of England daring the last genera- tion through the Girls' Friendly Society and he gave them all honour for their wide-awakeness and their great work in saving thousands upon thousands of girls from a bad life—girls who went from the country into the big cities and were opposed to all manner of danger —and in resisting the white slave tragic. He thought it a very great condemnation to them as Free Churches that they had not done as much as the Church of England in that respect, but a great work could be accomplished by means of girls' guilds if they were thoroughly in earnest about it. Then they wanted to gither up the young men as the strength and hope of the Churches in order that they might carry on the

BATTLE FOR RELTGtOBS FREBDOif, toleration, right and justice, and for the conversion of the world in the highest possible sense of the word. They must. therefore, form young men's auKiliaries in connection with the Free Church Council to familiarise them with Free Church principles and to lire their hearts with enthusiasm to emulate the great and wonder- ful work done by their forefathers. Dr. Townsend proceeded to enaphasise the value of each Council forming a united choir in order to familiarize the people with the highest forms of sacred music. He also urged the need for open air preaching. Is the past they had had a wrong idea with regard to open air preaching. They had always had the eloquent, forceful preacher in his pulpit whilst the young man serving the first quarter of his probation was sent to preach in the open air. (Applause). Mr. PARKER pointed out that in Warrington the Free Church Council had taken up the question of open air services and with great success. The Rev. S. Pm urged that it was unnecessary for a new organization to be formed to deal with the young

worry him. The more one got to know Browning, tag less one believed in his obscurity and yet there was an excuse for those who felt it. There was 9 certain roughness of speech which prevented his meaning being quite so clear as it might be. Then again, Browning was first of all a thinker. lie did not write to tire people and he was not giving pleasant relaxation for anyone who wanted it. Almost every lino he wrote was pregnant with thought. He wrote at white heat, in a perfect passion of created power. If they knew where to begin with Browning and how to read hirn,nor as a relaxation, but as an exercise as well as an artistic pleasure, they would get at the heart of him. The Lecturer also referred to Browning's genius in creating so many individual characters, to his eye for details. and his humour which was often uproarious. He WO quite certain Browning often took a delight in flingie something in the face of his reader which he knew ' reader would not care for.

Sunday will be a notable day in the history Wycliffe Congregational Church for it will witnb. the re-opening of the church which has been under-A going extensive renovation and re-decoration. Special, services will be held end sermons will be preached by the Rev. John Yonge: The musical portion of thei services will be of a festal character befitting they occasion and will include several anthems. In the afternoon Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac. will give an organ recital and this will be followed by an opea, meeting of the P.S.A. Society. The improvement which has been effected in the' appearance of the church is most marked. Thr exterior has been placed in a thorough state of repaii while the interior has been decorated in the mosti complete style. The ceiling has been painted in pale,' biscuit colour, lined in stencil, and the effect is vel striking. The terracotta walls form a 'pleasing contrast, while the pillars have been embellished irr green. The capiti.ls are worked in colours and Igilded, and harmonise very well with the general scheme. The rostrum and, in fact, all the wood work has been French polished, and the new carpets and umbrella stands are improvements which will be appreciated. The old lighting arrangements have been superseded by an installation of incandescent light. Another important part of the scheme is that the organ has been thoroughly overhauled and the instrument is now furnished with the most modern improvements ' including pneumatic action. All the ante-rooms have ' been re-cleaned and decorated. The total cost of the scheme is about £9OO. The work has been entrusted to Ur. C. H. Williams, decorator, of Altrincham, and has been admirably carried out under the supervision and to the designs of Mr. G. Faulkner Armit age, architect, of Stamford.

HARVEST FESTIVAL.

BEWSEY-ROAD WESLEYAN CHURCH. The harvest festival in connection with the above Church was held on Sunday. In spite of the inclement weather a good congregation assembled to hear the Rev. S. Pitt, the superintendent of the circuit, at the morning service. He took for his text the words : "The harvest truly is great, but the labourers are few. Pray ye, therefore, the Lord of the harvest that He send more labourers." The yearly miracle had he said been wrought again, and almost without knowing it, the harvest had arrived once more, and now they were able to see the results. This year they had had an especially good harvest, so much so that even the British farmer, who generally found something to grumble at, had been apparently satisfied with the yield. But they saw a lesson here not only of a physical but of a spiritual character. They had had a vast harvest, but how few were their labourers. In China the peoplo were dying at the rate of a million a month, and who was looking after their spiritual welfare ? He calculated that there were about 4,000 houses in their church's district inhabited by about 15,000 people. Hey/ many of those attended the house of God? In those homes might be found old scholars from their Sunday school, but who were now leading lives which were not at all consistent with their early training. How were they to reach these people. What they wanted was labourers who were not afraid of hard Work in the cause of the Master. But to obtain that they must pray—"pray ye,therefore,the Lord of the harvest." They must pray above all things, and when the final reaping came they would see the grand result of their labours. The choir rendered effectively the anthem, "0 Lord, how manifold are Thy works." In the even- ing the preacher was the Rev. %V. Mellor, who spoke on "The feeding of the five thousand." The antherli rendered was, " The eyes of all wait upon Theei (Elves.) The church had been tastefully decorate: with flowers and fruit and reflected great credit °- those who had had the work in hand. • STOCK AND SHARE MARKETS.

Art Exhibition at the School of Art.

Each year Schools of Art throughout the countrt are invited by the Government to submit student works of special excellence with a view to their being" considered in a National Competition. Those works ; gaining medals and prizes are lent by the Board of Education to the Managers of some of the Schools of Art. The Warrington Education Committee havo this year succeeded in their application for the loan. of a selection of these prize drawings, and they have arranged for them to be exhibited at the School of Art from the morning of Friday, the 28th September> ; until the evening of Tuesday, the 2nd October. Tbe, exhibition will be opened each morning at 10 arm° closed at 9 o'clock each evening. No charge will 0 ' made for 'admission to the exhibition. It is greatly to be hoped that lovers of Art and the public generally will avail themselves of this exceptional opportuni.tY of viewing some of the choicest works executed 10: art students as part of their course of instructioMt Art schools, and that an impetus will be given to tat' branch of the education of the town. .
